                                           Cubical residual complexes and profinite rigidity of real hyperbolic
                                                                        lattices
                                                                                             Yong Hou
arXiv:2609.16020v2 [math.GT] 30 Sep 2026




                                                                                              Abstract
                                                     We study profinite rigidity of real hyperbolic lattices, with the main application in dimension
                                                 three. The basic tool is a profinite version of the dual cube complex of a pair of filling curves
                                                 on a surface, which we call a profinite cubical residual complex. We prove that an equivariant
                                                 isomorphism between the completions of two such complexes is induced, after conjugation by a
                                                 single element, by an isomorphism of the discrete groups and complexes, and that the same holds
                                                 for free cocompact cellular actions of finitely generated residually finite groups on connected,
                                                 locally finite, finite dimensional regular CW complexes. On a closed orientable surface of genus
                                                 at least two, a theory of divisible fillings determines the completed cut tree of a nonseparating
                                                 simple closed curve. Combined with the homological intersection criterion of Boggi and Zalesskii,
                                                 this determines the completed dual square complex of a nonseparating filling pair, and hence
                                                 realizes every isomorphism of profinite surface groups which preserves the two marked cyclic
                                                 subgroups. In dimension three we combine these results with virtual fibering, profinite invariants
                                                 of hyperbolic 3-manifolds and the goodness of 3-manifold groups. Massey products and virtual
                                                 domination give cohomological integrality, and pseudo-Anosov dynamics, periodic orbit traces
                                                 and cross sections of suspension flows give a correspondence of prime periodic orbits together
                                                 with the fiber curves needed for the realization. Profinite rigidity of closed and of cusped lattices
                                                 follows, the cusped case through cyclic orbifold fillings, and we obtain Out(Γ) ∼= Out(Γ)b for every
                                                 lattice of PSL2 (C).

1    Introduction
For G a finitely generated residually finite group, its profinite completion G    b determines all finite
quotients of G [40]. However, it does not in general determine the dense copy of G inside G;    b in fact,
a completed action does not necessarily determine a discrete action of G, and one may ask when it
does. The purpose of this paper is to answer this question. We show how to determine discrete
actions from equivariant isomorphisms of profinite completions, and we apply this to the profinite
rigidity of hyperbolic lattices.
     Profinite rigidity of real hyperbolic lattices is a well known problem with both algebraic and
geometric origins [16, 49]. Our approach is to build spaces on which a profinite action can be
followed. We use Sageev’s cube complexes [43], and we generalize the construction of Guirardel’s
core [17], the core of a product of two trees, which Guirardel studied in connection with irreducible
outer automorphisms of free groups acting on R-trees, to the dual square complex of a divisible
filling pair of curves. With the resulting profinite cubical residual complexes (Definitions 1.4 and 1.6)
one can track a profinite action through the completion (Theorem 2.2) and then determine the
original G-action from it (Theorem 5.11). This last step works in every dimension (Theorem 2.3),
and we expect it to be useful for profinite rigidity in higher dimensions.
     In dimension two we work with nonseparating filling pairs of curves satisfying divisibility
conditions. Let S be a closed orientable surface of genus at least two, let Π = π1 (S), and let A = ⟨a⟩
be the cyclic subgroup represented by a nonseparating simple closed curve α. Cutting S along
α gives an efficient HNN splitting of Π whose vertex group H is the fundamental group of the
cut surface [44, 41]. Imposing the relation aℓn = 1, where ℓn = lcm(1, . . . , n), produces an HNN
extension with a finite edge group and a cocompact Fuchsian vertex group; we call these quotients
divisible fillings. A fixed point theorem for profinite Poincaré duality groups [50, Theorem 1.10
and Corollary 1.11] identifies the completed vertex group of each filling, and if Pα,ℓn denotes its
                    b then H = T
inverse image in Π,                 n≥3 Pα,ℓn . The conjugators appearing at the different levels can
be chosen compatibly, because the relevant sets form a nested family of nonempty compact sets,
and profinite malnormality [49, Theorem 3.3] detects adjacency in the profinite Bass–Serre tree.
In this way the divisible fillings determine the full profinite cut tree of α (Theorem 2.1). For an
ordered nonseparating filling pair (α, β), the dual square complex C(α, β) is the surface version of
the Guirardel core in Tα × Tβ . We complete its vertex, edge, square, endpoint occurrence and side
occurrence spaces, together with all attaching maps. Crossing of edges is detected in finite covers by
the homological criterion of Boggi and Zalesskii [5], and since this criterion is insensitive to replacing
the two cyclic generators by independent profinite unit powers, the two marked procyclic conjugacy
classes determine an equivariant isomorphism of the completed cores (Theorem 2.2).
     The original G-action is then determined from the completed occurrence structure. The point
is that once the profinite action sends some original vertex to an original vertex, the attaching
maps determine its original star; by connectedness this propagates through the whole complex and
also determines the dense acting group (Theorem 5.11). By induction over the skeleta the same
conclusion holds for free cocompact actions on connected, locally finite, finite dimensional regular
CW complexes (Theorem 2.3).
     3-dimensional hyperbolic geometry is one of the most developed theories in geometry, and
a tremendous amount of work has been done on the profinite rigidity question in dimension 3


                                                    2
[49, 50, 25]. What is so special about dimension 3 is the fact that all geometric structures are
determined by surfaces, as is already evident from the basic example of a mapping torus. It is
this special property that distinguishes dimension 3 from higher dimensions, and the theory of
hyperbolic 3-manifolds is especially successful because of it. In fact, we have a pretty much complete
understanding of 3-manifold groups through Perelman–Thurston geometrization [3], Agol’s virtual
fibration theorem [1, 2, 51], JSJ decompositions [23] and the surface subgroup theorem [24]. It is
safe to say that all the ingredients exist to establish profinite rigidity in this dimension, except the
realization part. Hence, by combining these high-powered results with our realization theorems, we
are able to complete the proof of profinite rigidity of lattices in PSL2 (C) (Theorem 2.9). Given an
isomorphism between the profinite completions of two finite volume hyperbolic 3-manifold groups,
we first produce surface curves to which the realization theorems apply. Liu’s regularity theorem [25,
Theorems 1.2–1.3], a triple Massey product evaluated on an explicit integral Heisenberg cocycle, and
the virtual domination theorem of Liu and Sun [26] give cohomological integrality (Theorem 7.4):
the cubic and quartic evaluations involve the same orientation unit but different powers of the
scalar µ, which forces µ = ±1. Comparing twisted torsion with fixed point traces [25, 22] and using
separation in finite quotients [21] together with compactness, we obtain a correspondence between
the prime periodic orbits of the two suspension flows. Two independent fibered classes place a
matched orbit in corresponding surface fibers: we perturb a class in the closure of a fibered cone
[1, 47], choose an orbit on which it vanishes [14, 11], and pass to characteristic simple elevations
[46]. Large pseudo-Anosov iterates then give two nonseparating filling pairs [27], and the realization
theorems apply (Corollary 10.4).
    In the cusped case, each peripheral map is a profinite unit times an integral map, so the closed
normal subgroups defining divisible peripheral fillings are preserved, because ⟨ekµ ⟩ = ⟨ek ⟩ for µ ∈ Z b ×.
The closed case, Mostow–Prasad rigidity and simultaneous drilling [6, 34, 39] give homeomorphisms
of the unfilled compact cores. Every fixed finite quotient factors through the fillings of all sufficiently
large index, only finitely many discrete outer classes occur, and the conjugator sets are compact, so
we determined the original cusped isomorphism (Corollary 11.4). Passing to finite extensions proves
Theorem 2.9, including Out(Γ) ∼  = Out(Γ) b for Kleinian lattices, and cusp integrality follows.
    Background on profinite groups, graphs of groups and their trees can be found in [44, 40, 41];
for regular CW complexes, PL maps and hyperbolic geometry see [18, 42, 47].
    The main results are collected in Section 2. Sections 3–6 contain the profinite preliminaries
and the realization theorems for cubical and graph residual complexes. Sections 7–11 prove the
cohomological, dynamical and filling results used for lattices. In Section 12 we treat marked
nonlattice groups and compact cores.

Definitions and notations
Definition. original G-object: Let G be a residually finite group and let Z be a left G-set with
finitely many G-orbits. Its profinite completion is Zb = limN ⊴ G N \Z, with the natural map
                                                             ←− f
ιZ : Z −→ Z, z 7−→ (N z)N ⊴f G . A point z ∈ Z is called original if zb ∈ ιZ (Z); equivalently, there is
             b                           b   b
z ∈ Z whose image N z is the N -th coordinate of zb for every N ⊴f G. This terminology is always
relative to the G-set Z in question. Whenever ιZ is injective, we identify Z with its original image.
Taking Z = G with the left multiplication action, the original elements of G  b are the elements of its
canonical subgroup G. For a completed complex, the definition applies separately to every cell and
occurrence space defined later, and the original complex with its original G-action is the discrete
complex with its discrete action. The same definition applies to completions formed over any cofinal
family of finite index normal subgroups.

Definition 1.1. A simple closed curve is the image of an embedding of a circle. It is nonseparating
if its complement in the surface is connected. Let α be such a curve on S. We write

                            Π = ⟨H, t | tA− t−1 = A+ ⟩,        H = π1 (Sα ),

where A± are the two cyclic boundary subgroups, A = A− , and t is the stable letter. We set
Sα = S \ int N (α), where N (α) is a closed annular neighborhood of α. This compact cut surface
is homotopy equivalent to S \ α. Based paths identify its fundamental group with the subgroup
H ≤ Π. We denote the original Bass–Serre tree by Tα . Efficiency of the splitting, defined below,
identifies H
           b and A                          b so the standard profinite tree, again denoted Tbα , has
                  b with their closures in Π,
vertex and edge spaces
                                 V (Tbα ) = Π/H,
                                            b        E(Tbα ) = Π/A.
                                                               b

The tree is the one associated to the completed HNN splitting before imposing any finite cyclic
curve power relation. We use full profinite cut tree only in this sense. See [44] and [41].

Definition 1.2. Let (G, Y ) be a finite graph of groups with residually finite fundamental group Γ.
The splitting is efficient when every vertex and edge group is closed in the profinite topology of Γ
and the topology induced on each of them is its full profinite topology. A graph of profinite groups
is proper if every vertex group maps injectively to its profinite fundamental group. See [41].

Definition 1.3. A cyclic subgroup is maximal cyclic if it is contained in no larger cyclic subgroup.
Given a nonseparating simple closed curve, let a be a based representative that traverses it once,
and set A = ⟨a⟩ ≤ Π. This subgroup is maximal cyclic. The marked pair is denoted by (Π,  b [A]). An
isomorphism of marked pairs is an isomorphism θ : Π1 → Π2 such that θ(A1 ) is conjugate to A2 .
                                                   b      b

Definition 1.4. Let C be a regular square (2-dimensional cube) complex. We choose for each cell
a characteristic map that is a homeomorphism onto its closure. We write C k for the set of open
k-cells. For an open edge e and an open square q, we fix the characteristic maps given by the cubical
structure,
                              χe : [−1, 1] −→ e,   χq : [−1, 1]2 −→ q.
For ϵ ∈ {−1, +1}, the pair (e, ϵ) is an endpoint occurrence of e. For j ∈ {1, 2} and ϵ ∈ {−1, +1}, set

                                 Fj,ϵ = {(x1 , x2 ) ∈ [−1, 1]2 : xj = ϵ}.

The triple (q, j, ϵ) is the corresponding side occurrence of q. We define

                              I10 (C) = {(e, ϵ) : e ∈ C 1 , ϵ ∈ {−1, +1}},

                       I21 (C) = {(q, j, ϵ) : q ∈ C 2 , j ∈ {1, 2}, ϵ ∈ {−1, +1}}.

where e is the unique open edge satisfying

Distinct occurrence orbits remain distinct even when their incident cell orbits coincide.
   Let C ′ be another regular square complex. A morphism F : I(C) → I(C ′ ) is a tuple of five
maps
                                     F = (F0 , F1 , F2 , F10 , F21 )
commuting with the four structure maps:

                                    F1 p10 = p′10 F10 , F0 q10 = q10

It is an isomorphism when its five component maps are bijective. In the profinite setting the
component maps are required to be continuous, and a continuous component-wise bijection is a
homeomorphism because the component spaces are compact and Hausdorff.

   A finite cubical residual diagram is a diagram of finite sets

                               I = C 0 , C 1 , C 2 , I10 , I21 , p10 , q10 , p21 , q21 ,
                                                                                     


with structure maps
                                 p10 : I10 −→ C 1 ,             q10 : I10 −→ C 0 ,
                                 p21 : I21 −→ C 2 ,             q21 : I21 −→ C 1 ,
which is isomorphic to a component-wise quotient H\I(C), where C is a regular square complex
and H acts cellularly on C with finitely many orbits on each cell and occurrence set. The quotient
structure maps are induced by those of I(C). An isomorphism here consists of bijections on the
five sets commuting with the four structure maps. Taking H = {1} includes the cubical residual
complex of every finite regular square complex.

    Completion is carried out separately for the occurrence spaces and for the cell spaces, so in
general there are no canonical identifications

                                    N \I10 (C) ∼
                                               = (N \C 1 ) × {−1, +1},
or
                                N \I21 (C) ∼
                                           = (N \C 2 ) × {1, 2} × {−1, +1},
because an element of N may reverse endpoint positions or permute side positions.
Example. Let C be the standard unit square tiling of R2 . We write

            vm,n = (m, n),         am,n = [m, m + 1] × {n},                    bm,n = {m} × [n, n + 1],

                                       qm,n = [m, m + 1] × [n, n + 1].
The translation group Γ = Z2 acts freely and cocompactly. The quotient cell sets contain one vertex
v, two edges a, b, and one square q, but

                                       Γ\I10 (C) = {a− , a+ , b− , b+ },

where
                      p10 (a± ) = a,       p10 (b± ) = b,           q10 (a± ) = q10 (b± ) = v.
For the square side occurrences,

                                    Γ\I21 (C) = {q1,− , q1,+ , q2,− , q2,+ },

with
             p21 (qj,ϵ ) = q,     q21 (q1,− ) = q21 (q1,+ ) = b,              q21 (q2,− ) = q21 (q2,+ ) = a.
The two vertical side positions are distinct in the quotient occurrence set although both map to b.
Likewise, the two horizontal positions are distinct although both map to a. Replacing the occurrence
sets by the relations
                                  {(a, v), (b, v)},    {(q, a), (q, b)}
would remove these multiplicities. We therefore take every inverse limit on the occurrence sets as
well as on the cell sets.

                                                           7
Definition 1.7. Let S be a connected closed orientable surface of genus ≥ 2 and let Π = π1 (S).
Let (α, β) be an ordered pair of nontrivial simple closed curves such that both S \ α and S \ β are
connected. We choose representatives such that

                                                 |α ∩ β| = i(α, β),

where i(α, β) is the minimum number of transverse intersections among representatives of their free
homotopy classes, called their geometric intersection number. Assume further that every component
of S \ (α ∪ β) is an open disk. Such an ordered pair is called a nonseparating filling pair.
    We choose a hyperbolic metric on S and replace α and β by their geodesic representatives. Let
                                                                  ∼ H2 . Elements of the same family
A and B be the Π-invariant families of their geodesic lifts in Se =
are disjoint, an element of A meets an element of B in at most one point, and A ∪ B is locally
finite.

    The graph α ∪ β has finitely many vertices and edges, and each complementary component is a
disk bounded by finitely many curve segments. Every component of the lifted complement is a lift
of one of these disks, so only finitely many wall segments meet it. A wall segment has two crossing

                                                         8
endpoints and belongs to two dual squares (Figure 1), so C(α, β) is a locally finite regular square
complex. The deck action preserves regions, wall segments, crossings, and face positions, so it is
cellular. The construction is the two dimensional case of Sageev’s cubulation of a wallspace [43, 20],
and for surfaces its image in the product of the two dual trees is the Guirardel core [17].

    Now let qx be the open dual square corresponding to a crossing x = Lα ∩ Lβ . The four regions
adjacent to x have coordinate pairs equal to the four vertices of

                                                Eα (Lα ) × Eβ (Lβ ).

                                                                9
Their cyclic order agrees with the boundary order of this product square, and the vertex assignment
extends to a unique cubical isomorphism
                                                ∼
                                                =
                                    j|qx : qx −
                                              → Eα (Lα ) × Eβ (Lβ ).

On each side, this is the affine map already defined on the corresponding dual edge, so the cell maps
agree on their common faces and define a continuous cellular map

                                          j : C(α, β) −→ Tα × Tβ .

   The action of Π preserves both wall families and their complementary components, hence for
every g ∈ Π we have
                                 j(gvR ) = j(vgR )
                                                                       
                                                = Uα (gR), Uβ (gR)
                                                                       
                                                = gUα (R), gUβ (R)
                                                = g j(vR ).
The action on the model cells is cubical, so uniqueness of the edge and square maps above yields

                               j(gz) = g j(z)        (g ∈ Π, z ∈ C(α, β)).

Therefore j is Π-equivariant, and the square at Lα ∩ Lβ maps to the product of the two dual tree
edges.
   Let N be a downward directed family of finite index normal subgroups of Π: for N1 , N2 ∈ N
there is N3 ∈ N with N3 ≤ N1 ∩ N2 . Order N by reverse inclusion. It is cofinal if

                                    ∀K ⊴f Π         ∃N ∈ N        N ≤ K.

We assume henceforth that N is cofinal among the finite index normal subgroups of Π. For
N ′ ≤ N , the covering projection N ′ \C → N \C induces a morphism N ′ \I(C) −→ N \I(C) on
all five component spaces. The profinite cubical residual completion with respect to N is the
component-wise inverse limit
                                    IbN (C) = lim N \I(C).
                                              ←−
                                                     N ∈N

Thus, for example:
                          CbN2 = lim N \C 2 ,        Ib21,N (C) = lim N \I21 (C),
                                 ←−                               ←−
                                N ∈N                                N ∈N

and the four structure maps are the inverse limits of the corresponding quotient maps. Let K be
the directed family of all finite index normal subgroups of Π. Restriction of coordinates defines an
isomorphism
                                    lim K\I(C) −→ lim N \I(C).
                                    ←−               ←−
                                   K∈K                     N ∈N

Now for the inverse map, take (xN )N ∈N a compatible point in the completion. Given K ∈ K ,
choose N ∈ N with N ≤ K and take the image of xN in K\I(C). This coordinate does not depend
on the choice of N , because the system is directed [40].
    In fact, we will show that the action of Π on each cell and occurrence set is free and has finitely
many orbits, see Proposition 5.1. Since Π is residually finite and N is cofinal, the coordinate maps
give injections with dense image

            C d ,→ CbNd    (0 ≤ d ≤ 2),       I10 (C) ,→ Ib10,N (C),       I21 (C) ,→ Ib21,N (C).

                                                      10
We call a point in one of these dense images, that is, a point coming from the discrete complex, an
original cell or an original occurrence.
    Choose base lifts αe ∈ A and βe ∈ B, with stabilizers A, B ≤ Π, and set

                                       Ω = {g ∈ Π : α
                                                    e ∩ g βe ̸= ∅}.

This set is A–B bi-invariant. If e = xA ∈ E(Tbα ) and f = yB ∈ E(Tbβ ), we define

                                     e⋔f       ⇐⇒        x−1 y ∈ Ω ⊆ Π.
                                                                     b

Then the condition does not depend on the coset representatives, because replacing x by xa and y
by yb, with a ∈ A and b ∈ B, replaces x−1 y by a−1 x−1 yb, while Ω is A–B bi-invariant. We call ⋔
the crossing relation. By Lemma 5.2, two edges cross exactly when the corresponding elevations
meet in every regular cover from a cofinal family.
   We next define the profinite cubical residual complex of the product of the two profinite trees.
For ξ ∈ {α, β}, let Vξ and Eξ be the vertex and edge spaces of Tbξ , and write ∂ξ,0 , ∂ξ,1 : Eξ → Vξ for
the two endpoint maps. Set

                      Jξ = Eξ × {0, 1},         pξ (e, r) = e,         qξ (e, r) = ∂ξ,r (e).

As {0, 1} is discrete, Jξ is profinite and both maps are continuous. The remaining component spaces
are
                P 0 = Vα × Vβ ,        P 1 = (Eα × Vβ ) ⊔ (Vα × Eβ ),  P 2 = Eα × Eβ ,
               I10 (P ) = (Jα × Vβ ) ⊔ (Vα × Jβ ),           I21 (P ) = (Jα × Eβ ) ⊔ (Eα × Jβ ).
On Jα × Vβ , we set
                            p10 (ι, v) = (pα (ι), v),        q10 (ι, v) = (qα (ι), v).
On Vα × Jβ , we set
                            p10 (v, ι) = (v, pβ (ι)),        q10 (v, ι) = (v, qβ (ι)).
On Jα × Eβ , we set
                            p21 (ι, e) = (pα (ι), e),        q21 (ι, e) = (qα (ι), e).
On Eα × Jβ , we set
                            p21 (e, ι) = (e, pβ (ι)),        q21 (e, ι) = (e, qβ (ι)).
All component spaces are profinite, and all four maps are continuous. We write I(Tbα × Tbβ ) for this
five component profinite cubical residual complex.
    The completed coordinate map is the unique continuous extension

                                       j : IbN (C) −→ I(Tbα × Tbβ )
                                       b

of the five component maps induced by j. Uniqueness follows from density of the original component
spaces and the Hausdorff property of the codomain spaces. Proposition 5.4 proves that these
component maps form an embedding and that the completed square space is

                               {e × f : e ∈ E(Tbα ), f ∈ E(Tbβ ), e ⋔ f }.

Example. Let x = α     e ∩ g βe be a crossing, let qx be its dual square, and let eα    e and egβe be the
corresponding tree edges. We fix a nested cofinal sequence Π ≥ N1 ≥ N2 ≥ · · · of finite index
normal subgroups and form the completion with respect to this sequence. Such a sequence exists
because a finitely generated group has only finitely many subgroups of each finite index: enumerate
the finite index normal subgroups    as L1 , L2 , . . . and set Nm = L1 ∩ · · · ∩ Lm . The original square
determined by qx is qbx = Nm qx m≥1 ∈ Cb 2 . Its four side positions give four elements
                                   

                       Nm (qx , j, ϵ) m≥1 ∈ Ib21 (C),        j ∈ {1, 2}, ϵ ∈ {−1, +1},

and the inverse limit map q21 sends them to the four boundary edge elements of qbx . Moreover,

                           eαe ⋔ egβe     ⇐⇒     g ∈ Ω,         j(qbx ) = eαe × egβe.
                                                                b


    Let γ ∈ Π.
            b For each m, choose gm ∈ Π representing the image of γ in Π/Nm . Then compatibility
of the quotient coordinates implies that γ qbx = Nm gm qx m≥1 is a well defined element of Cb 2 ,
                                                          

independent of the representatives gm , and by equivariance bj(γ qbx ) = γ eαe × egβe . If γ ∈
                                                                                             / Π, then

                                                            b        so an equality γ qbx = δ qbx with
δ ∈ Π would imply γ = δ. Hence left multiplication by an element of Π b \ Π preserves the completed
cubical residual complex but moves every square in this orbit outside the original subset. This is
why we need normalization in Theorem 5.11.
Definition 1.8. Given G finitely generated, a connected finite sheeted characteristic cover of a
connected aspherical space with fundamental group G is the cover corresponding to a finite index
characteristic subgroup of G. For m ≥ 1, we set Km (G) = [G:L]≤m L. If N ⊴f G and [G : N ] = d,
                                                          T

then Kd (G) ≤ N . We therefore obtain a cofinal family (Km (G))m≥1 among the finite index normal
subgroups of G, which we call the standard family of characteristic subgroups.
Definition 1.9. For n ≥ 1, we set ℓn = lcm(1, 2, . . . , n). Then

                            ℓn | ℓn+1 ,       n ≥ d =⇒ d | ℓn ,          ℓn −→ ∞.

Indeed, ℓn+1 = lcm(ℓn , n + 1), the integer d occurs among 1, . . . , n whenever n ≥ d, and ℓn ≥ n. For
a marked element a, the quotient obtained by imposing aℓn = 1 is called the nth divisible filling. In
the simultaneous cusped construction, the orders ri ℓn , with fixed positive integers ri , are called the
corresponding divisible filling orders.
Definition 1.10. Let (ϕt )t∈R be a flow without stationary points on a manifold M . A point x ∈ M
is periodic if ϕT (x) = x for some T > 0. Its least period is

                                        Tx = min{T > 0 : ϕT (x) = x}.

For a homeomorphism f : S → S, a point x ∈ S has least return period m ≥ 1 if f m (x) = x and
f j (x) ̸= x for 1 ≤ j < m. A positive periodic parametrization of the flow orbit through x is the map

                              γx : R/Tx Z −→ M,              γx ([s]Tx ) = ϕs (x).

A positively oriented closed suspension trajectory is prime if it is not the r-fold traversal of another
positively oriented closed suspension trajectory for any integer r ≥ 2. For a positive periodic
parametrization γ : R/T Z → M , its r-fold traversal is the map

                           γ [r] : R/(rT )Z −→ M,            γ [r] ([s]rT ) = γ([s]T ).

                                                        12
Let K be a group and let b ∈ K. The element b is a proper power in K if b = g r for some g ∈ K
and some integer r ≥ 2. Thus b is not a proper power in K if no such pair (g, r) exists.
    The group matters here, since roots may appear after enlarging the group: if G = ⟨a⟩ ∼  = Z and
H = ⟨a2 ⟩ ≤ G, then a2 is not a proper power in H but is a square in G. Later the same element
will lie in a fiber subgroup, in a finite index subgroup and in a mapping torus group, and we always
say in which group a statement about proper powers is meant. Dynamical primeness is a different
condition; for pseudo-Anosov suspension orbits, the implication we need is Lemma 8.1.
Definition 1.11. Let M be a connected manifold. An integral class χ ∈ H 1 (M, Z) = Hom(π1 M, Z)
is primitive if χ(π1 M ) = Z. A primitive integral class χ is fibered if it is induced by a fibration
M → S 1 with connected fiber. Its fiber group is Πχ = ker χ. See [47, Section 3] for fibered classes
in dimension three. The class χ is primitive, hence surjective, and there is an exact sequence
                                                         χ
                                   1 −→ Πχ −→ π1 M −→ Z −→ 1.

We choose an element t ∈ π1 M such that χ(t) = 1 ∈ Z. The kernel Πχ is normal, so conjugation by
t restricts to an automorphism

                                 aχ : Πχ −→ Πχ ,        aχ (x) = txt−1 ,

and the choice of t splits the sequence:

                                   π1 M ∼
                                        = Πχ ⋊aχ ⟨t⟩,        χ(t) = 1.

If t′ is another element with χ(t′ ) = 1, then h = t′ t−1 ∈ Πχ and

                                    conjt′ |Πχ = Inn(h) ◦ conjt |Πχ .

Consequently the outer class [aχ ] ∈ Out(Πχ ) is independent of the chosen element t with χ(t) = 1
and is the algebraic monodromy of the oriented fibered class χ. Choosing t with χ(t) = −1 instead
would replace this outer class by its inverse. The condition χ(t) = 1 fixes the orientation determined
by χ.
Definition 1.12. Let X be a finite dimensional regular CW complex of dimension d with a
characteristic homeomorphism χσ for each open k-cell σ. A codimension one face position of σ is
an open (k − 1)-cell ι of the pulled back boundary decomposition. Its image

                                           τ = χσ (ι) ∈ X k−1

is the corresponding open codimension one face of σ. An occurrence of τ as a face of σ is a face
                                                                       ∼
                                                                       =
position ι with χσ (ι) = τ , or equivalently the restriction χσ |ι : ι −
                                                                       → τ . Thus an occurrence contains
both the incident face and its position in the model boundary.
    For 0 ≤ k ≤ d, write X k for the set of open k-cells. If 1 ≤ k ≤ d, set
                                                                                          
                         
                                   σ ∈ X k , τ ∈ X k−1 ,                              
                                                                                       
           Ik,k−1 (X) = (σ, τ, ι) : ι ⊂ S k−1  is a codimension one face position of σ, .
                                                                                      
                                   χσ (ι) = τ                                         

We keep the two projections pk,k−1 (σ, τ, ι) = σ and qk,k−1 (σ, τ, ι) = τ . The full cellular residual
complex I• (X) consists of all cell spaces X k , all codimension one occurrence spaces Ik,k−1 (X), and all
maps pk,k−1 , qk,k−1 . If X is a square complex, this structure contains the complex of Definition 1.4,
with endpoint and side occurrence spaces I1,0 and I2,1 .

                                                   13
    A cellular automorphism g acts on face positions by

                          g(σ, τ, ι) = gσ, gτ, ι′ ,             ι′ = (χgσ )−1 gχσ (ι) .
                                                                                         


The set ι′ is the unique face position in the chosen model of gσ that maps to gτ . If a subgroup N
acts cellularly on X, define the quotient cellular residual complex component-wise by

                                           I• (N \X) := N \I• (X).

Two face position orbits remain distinct unless an element of N identifies them, even when their
image cells in N \X coincide.
    Suppose a finitely generated residually finite group Γ acts freely, cellularly, and cocompactly on
a connected, locally finite, finite dimensional regular CW complex X. For a cofinal family N of
finite index normal subgroups of Γ, we define

                                          Ib• (X) = lim I• (N \X),
                                                    ←−
                                                       N ∈N

where the inverse limit is taken simultaneously on every cell space, every occurrence space, and
every structure map. An isomorphism of completed full cellular residual complexes consists of
homeomorphisms on these profinite spaces that commute with all structure maps and admit an
inverse with the same property.


2    Main theorems
We first state the cut tree theorem, then the realization theorems for cubical residual complexes,
and finally the applications: profinite rigidity of lattices in PSL2 (C), and realization statements in
arbitrary dimension relative to the family of characteristic subgroups.

                                                   17
    At any one level in (2.3), quotienting the equivariant isomorphism by Gi /Kmj (Gi ) produces
an isomorphism of the full residual complexes of the base triangulations C1 and C2 . Their face
relations determine the simplices and hence give a simplicial isomorphism C1 → C2 . The cofinal
family is used to obtain a realization whose induced isomorphism on profinite completions agrees
with the given Φ up to inner conjugation.

Theorem 2.7. Let S be a closed orientable surface of genus at least two, Π = π1 (S), and let
(α, β) be an ordered nonseparating filling pair with maximal cyclic subgroups A, B ≤ Π. For every
continuous automorphism θ ∈ Aut(Π) b satisfying

                     θ(A) = xAx−1 ,      θ(B) = yBy −1       for some x, y ∈ Π,

there exist h ∈ Aut(Π) and u ∈ Π  b such that θ = Inn(u) ◦ h.
                                                            b The two conjugacy conditions are
independent of the representative θ of its outer automorphism class.

Remark 2.8. The surface markings are conjugacy classes of closed procyclic subgroups, and no
generator is chosen. With this marking, the given profinite isomorphism is realized after conjugation
by an element of Πb 2 . The example in Remark 5.12 shows that this conjugation cannot be omitted.


Profinite rigidity for 3-dimensional hyperbolic lattices
In Sections 7–11, we construct the surface markings required for the lattice theorem.

Theorem 2.9. Let Γ, ∆ < PSL2 (C) be lattices, with torsion allowed. Every continuous isomorphism
  b→∆
Φ:Γ   b has the form

                                                                   ∼
                                                                   =
                            Φ = Inn(u) ◦ fb,     u ∈ ∆,
                                                     b       f :Γ−
                                                                 → ∆.

The isomorphism f is induced, up to a discrete inner automorphism, by an isometry of the corre-
sponding finite volume hyperbolic orbifolds, possibly reversing orientation. Consequently lattices in
PSL2 (C) with isomorphic profinite completions are isomorphic, and completion induces
                                                 ∼
                                                 =
                                        Out(Γ) −
                                               → Out(Γ).
                                                     b

    In the closed case, we combine the integral sign theorem, the prime orbit correspondence, and
the two fibration configuration of Theorems 7.4, 8.4 and 9.5 and Corollary 9.6. The one orbit
theorem, Theorem 10.3, then invokes the two curve criterion. In the cusped case we use the unit
invariant filling argument of Corollary 11.4. Peripheral integrality follows from Corollary 11.5.




                                                 18
2.1   Applications to nonlattices
The graph theorem does not require a geometric action on hyperbolic space, and Corollary 12.1
therefore applies to every finitely generated torsion-free Kleinian group through a compact core graph
that is surjective on fundamental groups. Directed Cayley graphs allow torsion in Corollary 12.2.
In both applications, the finite quotient maps are not assumed to be compatible with each other at
the outset.
    The boundary subgroup conclusion of Theorem 12.5 allows compressible boundary and determines
the images of the boundary fundamental groups. If we keep the full cellular residual complex,
Corollaries 12.3 and 12.6 instead give PL homeomorphisms of the given compact cores and core
pairs. For incompressible boundary with the full induced profinite topology, Corollary 12.7 applies
the divisible filling and filling core theorems to marked boundary filling pairs.


3     Profinite preliminaries
We collect some elementary facts about profinite groups in the form in which they will be used.
Most of them can be found in the references cited; a few need minor modifications, and for these we
give proofs.

Lemma 3.2. Let G be residually finite, let H ≤ G have finite index, and let A ≤ H admit a
retraction r : H → A. Then A is closed in the profinite topology of G, and the topology induced from
G on A is the full profinite topology of A.
Proof. Closedness of a virtual retract in a residually finite group is [31, Lemma 2.2]. We prove that
the induced topology is the full profinite topology.
   We fix N ⊴f A. The subgroup r−1 (N ) has finite index in H and satisfies r−1 (N ) ∩ A = N . Let

                              D = coreG (r−1 (N )) =         gr−1 (N )g −1 .
                                                       \

                                                       g∈G

Only finitely many conjugates occur because r−1 (N ) has finite index, so D ⊴f G. Moreover,
D ∩ A ≤ r−1 (N ) ∩ A = N , so every basic neighborhood N of 1 in the full profinite topology of A
contains the intersection with A of an open normal subgroup of G. The reverse inclusion follows
because the intersection with A of a finite index subgroup of G has finite index in A. Therefore the
induced and full profinite topologies on A coincide.

Theorem 3.6 (Niblo-Wilton). Let Π be the fundamental group of a connected compact surface with
negative Euler characteristic, and let P, Q ≤ Π be finitely generated. Then:

  (i) every double coset P gQ is separable in Π,

 (ii) in the profinite completion,
                                                P ∩ Q = P ∩ Q.




                                                    21
Proof. Part (i) is Niblo’s theorem [36]. For orientable compact surfaces, we use its precise formulation
in [48, Theorem 3.3], which yields separability of P (gQg −1 ), and hence of its right translate P gQ.
For completeness, if the surface is nonorientable, let Π0 ⊴ Π be the subgroup of its orientable double
cover. We set P0 = P ∩ Π0 and Q0 = Q ∩ Π0 . Choose finite coset decompositions P = i pi P0 and
                                                                                             S
     S
Q = j Q0 qj . Then
                                     P gQ = pi P0 (gQ0 g −1 ) gqj .
                                             [                

                                             i,j

The two factors inside the parentheses are finitely generated subgroups of Π0 . Their product is
closed in Π0 by the orientable case. Because Π0 is closed in Π and inherits its full profinite topology,
each of these translates is closed in Π. The finite union is therefore closed. Part (ii) follows from
Minasyan’s criterion for intersections of subgroup closures for groups with separable double cosets
[32, Corollary 1.2].

Theorem 3.7. Let G be either the fundamental group of a connected closed orientable surface of
genus at least two or the fundamental group of an orientable torsion-free finite volume hyperbolic
3-manifold. Then we have:

  (i) The group G is hereditarily conjugacy separable.

 (ii) For every g ∈ G,
                                                   CGb(g) = CG (g),

 (iii) In the case of surface groups, for every pair of finitely generated subgroups P, Q ≤ G, every
       double coset P gQ is separable and

                                               P ∩ Q = P ∩ Q.

      In the finite volume hyperbolic 3-manifold case, the same two conclusions hold for every pair
      of finitely generated abelian subgroups P, Q ≤ G.

 (iv) If g ∈ G is loxodromic and is not a proper power in G, then ⟨g⟩ is a virtual retract, is closed,
      and inherits its full profinite topology from G.

Proof. For surface groups, hereditary conjugacy separability follows from Martino [28, Theorem 3.7],
since every subgroup of finite index is again a surface group. For finite volume hyperbolic 3-manifold
groups it follows from Hamilton–Wilton–Zalesskii [21, Theorem 1.3]. Item (ii) is Minasyan [30,
Corollary 12.3]. The statement about double cosets in surface groups is Theorem 3.6(i), and the
3-manifold statement is Hamilton–Wilton–Zalesskii [21, Theorem 1.4]. In the surface case, the
identity for intersections of subgroup closures follows from Theorem 3.6. In the 3-manifold case,
let P, Q be finitely generated abelian and let L ≤ G have finite index with P ∩ Q ≤ L. Then
P ∩ L is finitely generated abelian, so (P ∩ L)Q is separable by the same theorem about double
cosets. By the pairwise criterion [32, Theorem 1.1], P ∩ Q = P ∩ Q. In the Kleinian case item (iv)
follows from the alternative between a virtual retract and a virtual fiber subgroup [3, (H.9) and
(L.15)]. The virtual fiber alternative cannot hold for ⟨g⟩. Indeed, a fiber in a finite cover of a finite
volume hyperbolic 3-manifold has negative Euler characteristic, and every finite index subgroup of
its fundamental group is nonabelian. Such a group cannot be commensurable with an infinite cyclic
group. In the surface case item (iv) follows from quasiconvex virtual retraction. Closedness and
fullness then follow from Lemma 3.2.




                                                      22
Lemma 3.8. Let G be either the fundamental group of a connected closed orientable surface of
genus at least two or the fundamental group of an orientable torsion-free finite volume hyperbolic
3-manifold, regarded as a dense subgroup of G.
                                            b Then

                 NGb(G) = G,         Z(G)

Proof. These facts are elementary, but we did not find a reference for exactly this form, so we give
the proof. All closures in this proof are taken in G.
                                                   b We use Theorem 3.7(i)–(iii).
    First choose loxodromic elements x, y ∈ G, neither of which is a proper power, whose axes are
distinct. Such elements exist because G is a non-elementary torsion-free discrete group of orientation
preserving isometries of H2 or H3 . In either case, the centralizer in G of a loxodromic element is
infinite cyclic. Choosing generators of these centralizers, we therefore have

                                   CG (x) = ⟨x⟩,          CG (y) = ⟨y⟩.

Moreover,
                                           ⟨x⟩ ∩ ⟨y⟩ = {1}.

of a loxodromic element has the same axis as that element.
    By Theorem 3.7(ii),
                                   CGb(x) = ⟨x⟩,    CGb(y) = ⟨y⟩.
By part (iii) of the same theorem,

                                     ⟨x⟩ ∩ ⟨y⟩ = ⟨x⟩ ∩ ⟨y⟩ = {1}.

                                                     

                                        b−1 h ∈ CGb(y) = ⟨y⟩.

Theorem 3.9. Every orientable finite volume hyperbolic 3-manifold group has a finite index subgroup
that is the fundamental group of a compact special cube complex. If a torsion-free group has such a
finite index subgroup, then its profinite completion is torsion-free.

Proof. For closed hyperbolic 3-manifolds, virtual compact specialness follows from [2, Theorems 1.1
and 9.3]. For cusped finite volume hyperbolic 3-manifolds, it is Wise’s theorem in the form stated in
[49, Theorem 9.2]. The assertion that a torsion-free virtually compact special group has torsion-free
profinite completion is [49, Proposition 3.2].

Theorem 3.10. Let G be a hyperbolic virtually compact special group and let H, K ≤ G be infinite
quasiconvex subgroups. Regard G as a dense subgroup of G.
                                                       b If

                                 K = zHz −1        for some z ∈ G,
                                                                b

then there is g ∈ G such that K = gHg −1 .

Proof. The infinite quasiconvex subgroup case of Chagas–Zalesskii [8] states that two nonconjugate
infinite quasiconvex subgroups have nonconjugate images in some finite quotient of G. Suppose that
H and K were not conjugate in G, and choose such a quotient q : G → Q. Its continuous extension
     b → Q satisfies
qb : G
                                     q(K) = qb(z)q(H)qb(z)−1 ,
because Q is finite and qb(H) = q(H) and qb(K) = q(K). This contradicts the choice of q and proves
the claim.

Definition. A profinite graph is a profinite space T = V (T ) ⊔ E(T ) with closed vertex set V (T )
and continuous endpoint maps d0 , d1 : T → V (T ) restricting to the identity on V (T ). We set
E ∗ (T ) = T /V (T ), with distinguished point ∗. It is a profinite tree if
                                       ∗             ∂            ϵ
                             0 −→ Z[[E
                                  b      (T ), ∗]] −
                                                   → Z[[V
                                                     b    (T )]] →
                                                                 − Z
                                                                   b −→ 0

is exact, where ∂(e) = d1 (e) − d0 (e) and ϵ(v) = 1. The double brackets denote inverse limits of free
modules on finite quotient sets, with the distinguished point omitted in the pointed case. See [50].
Theorem 4.1 (Ribes). Let (G, Y ) be a finite connected efficient graph of groups with fundamental
group Γ. Fix a maximal subtree Y0 ⊂ Y and use the corresponding vertex embeddings and stable
letters in the discrete and profinite presentations. The continuous homomorphism from the profinite
fundamental group of the graph of completed groups to Γ b that extends the completed vertex embeddings
and sends each stable letter to the image of the corresponding discrete stable letter is an isomorphism.
If every edge group is finite, the completed graph of groups is proper and its standard profinite
Bass–Serre tree has the coset description: vertex and edge stabilizers are the corresponding conjugates.
    For the proof see [41].
    The HNN splitting (4.1) obtained by cutting the surface is efficient. Indeed, H and A± are
finitely generated quasiconvex subgroups of the hyperbolic surface group Π. Surface groups are
virtually compact special, so the virtual retraction theorem for quasiconvex subgroups makes these
subgroups retracts of finite index subgroups of Π [19, 51]. By Lemma 3.2, they are closed in the
profinite topology and inherit their full profinite topologies. The standard completion theorem for
finite efficient graphs of groups then identifies the profinite fundamental group of the completed
HNN splitting with Π  b [41]. In particular, the full profinite cut tree Tbα used below is well defined
and has vertex and edge spaces Π/Hb     and Π/A,
                                             b     respectively.
Definition. Let p be a prime, n ≥ 0 an integer, and P a profinite group. We write Zp [[P ]] =
limr,U (Z/pr Z)[P/U ], where r ≥ 1 and U ranges over open normal subgroups of P . The group P is
←−
a profinite P Dn group at p if the trivial module Zp has a resolution by finitely generated projective
profinite Zp [[P ]]-modules, cdp (P ) = n, and
                       H j (P, Zp [[P ]]) = 0   (j ̸= n),    H n (P, Zp [[P ]]) ∼
                                                                                = Zp
as abelian groups. Cohomology here is continuous cohomology with profinite coefficients. This is
the definition of [50].
Theorem 4.2. Let V be a non-elementary cocompact Fuchsian group. Every continuous action
of Vb on a profinite tree, in the sense of [50, Definition 1.2], with finite edge stabilizers has a fixed
vertex.
     In addition, in the standard tree of a proper finite graph of profinite groups with finite edge
groups, the intersection of two distinct vertex stabilizers is finite. More precisely, it is contained in
the stabilizer of every edge in the smallest profinite subtree joining the two vertices.

                                                     25
Proof. By Selberg’s lemma, we choose a torsion-free normal subgroup Σ ⊴f V . As V is cocompact
Fuchsian, there is a closed orientable hyperbolic surface SΣ with Σ = π1 (SΣ ). A finite index
subgroup is closed and inherits its full profinite topology from the group, so the closure of Σ in Vb is
an open normal subgroup canonically identified with Σ.      b
                          2
    The group Σ is a P D group, that is, a Poincaré duality group of dimension 2 [7]. It is good in
the sense of Serre because it is the fundamental group of the compact 3-manifold SΣ × I [3]. By
[50, Theorem 1.10], Σb is a profinite P D 2 group at every prime. In particular, cdp (Σ)b = 2 for every
prime p.
    The group Σb is torsion-free, for if Σ
                                         b contained an element of finite order, then it would contain a
closed subgroup Cp of prime order for some p. By monotonicity of p-cohomological dimension for
closed subgroups we have
                                          cdp (Cp ) ≤ cdp (Σ)
                                                           b = 2,

which contradicts cdp (Cp ) = ∞ [40]. Hence Σ
                                            b has no nontrivial finite subgroup.
                                        b For every edge e of the profinite tree,
   We now restrict the given action to Σ.

                                         b (e) = Σ ∩ StabVb (e) = 1,
                                     StabΣ       b

because the Vb -stabilizer of e is finite and Σ
                                              b is torsion-free. Thus every edge stabilizer of the restricted
action has cohomological dimension 0 = 2 − 2. Corollary 1.11 of Wilton–Zalesskii [50] applies to
this good P D2 group and shows that
                                                    T Σ ̸= ∅.
                                                      b

It follows that Σ  b has a fixed vertex. Alternatively, one can apply [50, Theorem 1.7], since the
restricted edge stabilizers have cohomological dimension 0 < 1.
    In fact, this fixed vertex is unique. Suppose that Σ       b fixed two distinct vertices. Then for
each h ∈ Σ, by [41, Theorem 4.1.5] its fixed point set is a closed profinite subtree, so by [41,
            b
Proposition 2.4.9] it contains the smallest profinite subtree joining those vertices. Hence every
element of Σ b fixes this subtree pointwise. The subtree contains an edge, so Σ b would be contained in
an edge stabilizer, which is trivial for the restricted action. This is impossible because the residually
finite infinite group Σ embeds densely in Σ.    b Thus T Σ  b
                                                               is a singleton. As Σb ⊴ Vb , the group Vb
preserves this singleton and therefore fixes its unique vertex.
    Now let T be the standard tree of a proper finite graph of profinite groups with finite edge
groups, and let v = ̸ w be distinct vertices. Let U be the smallest profinite subtree containing v and
w. Since U contains two distinct vertices, it contains an edge e. By the same two results from [41],
every element fixing both v and w fixes U pointwise. Hence, Stab(v) ∩ Stab(w) ≤ Stab(e). The edge
stabilizer is finite, which proves the claim.

    Our first lemma is a routine consequence of the orbifold presentation and of profinite Bass–
Serre theory; we prove the HNN identification and efficiency, after which the completion and tree
statements follow from [41, Proposition 6.5.3 and Corollary 6.3.6].

Since the defining relations show that the orders of c− and c+ divide n, and the assignments

                                    c− 7−→ 1,           c+ 7−→ 1,            xj , yj 7−→ 0

define a homomorphism Vα,n → Z/nZ, both c− and c+ have order at least n, and hence n. Moreover,
                                                        1                                  2
                                                                        
                            χorb = 2 − 2(g − 1) − 2 1 −                      = 2 − 2g +      < 0.
                                                        n                                  n

Replacing c+ by c−1
                  + in the vertex presentation turns it into the usual oriented orbifold presentation
of signature (g − 1, n, n). Thus Vα,n is a non-elementary cocompact Fuchsian group. Abelianizing
the full HNN presentation, we find

                                              (Γα,n )ab ∼

This agrees with imposing n[a] = 0 on the primitive class [a] ∈ H1 (S, Z).
   Next we show efficiency. We first prove that the vertex group inherits its full profinite topology.
For this argument, write
                                     V = Vα,n ,     Γ = Γα,n .

normal subgroup of V . Conversely, the construction above shows that every N ⊴f V contains such
an intersection. These two neighborhood bases are therefore cofinal, so the induced topology on V

                                                         30
Theorem 4.9 (Wilton–Zalesskii). Let G be word-hyperbolic and suppose that G is the fundamental
group of a compact virtually special cube complex. If {H1 , . . . , Hr } is a malnormal family of
quasiconvex subgroups of G, then {H 1 , . . . , H r } is a malnormal family in G.
                                                                               b

    This is [49, Theorem 3.3].
Definition ([49]). For an integer k ≥ 0, an action of a profinite group P on a profinite tree T is
k-acylindrical if, for every 1 ̸= g ∈ P and every two vertices v, w fixed by g, dT (v, w) ≤ k. Here
dT (v, w) is the number of edges in a shortest finite edge path joining v and w, and is infinite if no
such path exists. This is [49, Definition 7.2].
   Profinite malnormality [49, Theorem 3.3] and the acylindricity criterion for a graph of profinite
groups with one edge [49, Lemma 7.3] are the two ingredients of the next lemma; the criterion for
adjacency comes from the edge stabilizers being infinite procyclic.
Lemma 4.10. The action of Π       b on the full cut tree Tbα is 1-acylindrical. Consequently, for two
distinct vertices v, w ∈ V (Tα ),

In the adjacent case this intersection is the infinite procyclic stabilizer of their common edge.
Proof. The subgroup A = ⟨a⟩ is maximal cyclic in the torsion-free hyperbolic surface group Π.
Hence it is quasiconvex and malnormal. Surface groups are fundamental groups of compact special

(4.3)                              A ∩ zAz −1 = 1       (z ∈ Π

the edge group is malnormal in the profinite fundamental group. Wilton–Zalesskii’s standard
profinite Bass–Serre acylindricity lemma [49, Lemma 7.3] therefore shows that the action on Tbα is

    Let v ̸= w. If they are adjacent, then their unique common edge e satisfies

                                    Stab(v) ∩ Stab(w) = Stab(e).

Indeed, an element fixing both endpoints fixes the smallest profinite subtree joining them, which
here is the single edge e with its endpoints, and its stabilizer is a conjugate of A ∼
                                                                                     =Zb and is infinite.
    Conversely, suppose that 1 ̸= z ∈ Stab(v) ∩ Stab(w). By [41, Theorem 4.1.5], the nonempty
fixed subgraph U = (Tbα )z is a profinite subtree. The coset description of the edges and (4.3) show
that two distinct edges have trivially intersecting stabilizers, so U has at most one edge.
    Exactness of the sequence defining a profinite tree implies that an edge-free profinite tree has
only one vertex. As v, w ∈ V (U ) are distinct, U has one edge, say with endpoints v0 , v1 . Then it
has no further vertex, for an additional vertex u could be separated from the finite set {v0 , v1 } by a
continuous function

                       f : V (U ) −→ F2 ,     f (u) = 1,     f (v0 ) = f (v1 ) = 0.

The induced continuous homomorphism Z[[Vb    (U )]] → F2 would vanish on the image of the boundary
mapping but not on the augmentation-zero element u − v0 , contradicting exactness. Therefore v, w
are the endpoints of the unique edge of U , so they are adjacent.

    The vertex space of the cut tree is Π   b i /H i , so this equality induces a continuous equivariant
bijection on vertices. Since two distinct vertices are adjacent when their stabilizers have infinite
intersection, by Lemma 4.10 this condition is preserved by θ. An adjacent pair determines a unique
edge, whose stabilizer is the intersection of the two vertex stabilizers and hence a conjugate of Ai , so
Theorem 4.11 extends the vertex map uniquely to an equivariant isomorphism of the profinite trees.
    Every subgroup used here is obtained from the marked procyclic subgroup by taking power
subgroups, closed normal closures, quotient vertex groups, and intersections. So a marked profinite
isomorphism sends the completed cut surface vertex group to the corresponding conjugacy class and
induces the tree isomorphism. We now check that the result does not depend on the conjugator.
For either cut tree, let v be the vertex with stabilizer H. Then

                                                     NΠ
                                                      b (H) = H.

Indeed, if n normalizes H, then v and nv have the same stabilizer H. If they were distinct,
Lemma 4.10 would make them adjacent, and their stabilizer intersection would be a procyclic edge
stabilizer. This is impossible because H is a nonabelian free group embedded in H. So nv = v, and
n ∈ H. The reverse inclusion is trivial.

   With endpoint positions labelled 0 and 1, by Theorem 4.11 each tree map either preserves both

one of its two factors interchanges them and the other preserves them.

Left multiplication by x commutes with the endpoint maps, so F has the same compatibility with
endpoint occurrences as F ′ . The uniqueness argument applies to F , so the identity and composition
conclusions also hold for the original marked isomorphisms.


5    Profinite cubical residual complex
Filling core
Let (α, β) be a nonseparating filling pair on S, and set Π = π1 (S). We write Tα , Tβ for the original
cut trees. In Se ∼
                 = H2 , the lifts of α and β form locally finite families, and the geodesics in each
family are pairwise disjoint.
Proposition 5.1. The geometric dual square complex C = C(α, β) is connected, locally finite, and
regular, and Π acts freely, without inversions, and cocompactly. There is a Π-equivariant cellular
embedding
                                          j : C ,→ Tα × Tβ
whose squares are the products of crossing tree edges.

Proof. Step 1. A vertex of C(α, β) is a complementary region R of the full wall arrangement in
Se ∼
   = H2 . The region R lies in one component Uα (R) of the complement of the α-walls and one
component Uβ (R) of the complement of the β-walls. These components are the vertices of Tα and
Tβ , so set j(R) = (Uα (R), Uβ (R)). A dual edge changes one coordinate, and a dual square at a
crossing changes both. This defines a cellular Π-equivariant map j : C → Tα × Tβ .
Step 2. Each component of the complement of one wall family is convex, so the intersection of a
chosen α-component and a chosen β-component is connected. Two complementary regions with the
same pair of coordinates must therefore coincide. Since an edge dual to a segment of an α-wall L
has coordinates consisting of the tree edge represented by L and a component Uβ of the complement
of the β-walls, the segment is exactly L ∩ Uβ . Convexity makes this intersection connected, so
the coordinates determine at most one such edge. The same argument applies with the two wall
families interchanged. A product eα × eβ determines one lifted geodesic from each family; these two
geodesics meet in at most one point, so the product determines at most one square, and it does so
exactly when they cross. Hence each edge and square maps homeomorphically onto its product cell.
Together with injectivity on vertices, this proves cellular injectivity. Since each closed edge and

                                                 34
each closed square maps homeomorphically onto its product cell, its characteristic map is injective
on the boundary, hence C is regular. For surfaces, this determines Guirardel’s core [17].
Step 3. Any two complementary regions can be joined by a compact path in H2 transverse to the
walls and avoiding their crossing points. Local finiteness implies that the path crosses only finitely
many wall segments. The dual edges of these segments form an edge path in C, so C is connected.
Cutting the compact surface along the finite filling graph α ∪ β produces finitely many compact
polygonal disks, each with finitely many sides. The map of each disk lifts to H2 and its interior
maps onto a lifted complementary region, so the closure of that region is contained in the compact
image of the disk and has finitely many sides. So every lifted complementary region is bounded
and finitely sided, and every wall segment, lying in the boundary of an adjacent such region, is also
bounded. The quotient of C is the finite square cellulation dual to the filling graph. Finite sidedness
implies local finiteness, and the finite quotient makes the action cocompact. If a deck transformation
stabilizes a vertex, edge, or square, it preserves respectively a bounded complementary polygon,
a bounded wall segment, or a crossing point in H2 . Such an isometry is elliptic. But the surface
group is torsion-free and acts freely on H2 , so the stabilizer is trivial. Thus the action is free and
without inversions.
    Finally, give every tree edge length one. The coordinate maps of j are continuous on every
closed cell of C, so j is continuous for both the product metric topology and the product of the CW
topologies. Fix x ∈ C. Around each coordinate of j(x) choose a small open interval contained in its
open edge, or, if the coordinate is a vertex, the open star truncated at distance 1/2. Their product
U is open for both topologies. Every product cell meeting U contains j(x) in its closure. Since j is
injective and maps each closed cell onto the corresponding closed product cell, we have

                                        j −1 (U ) ⊆ Kx :=
                                                             [
                                                                     σ.
                                                            σ: x∈σ

Local finiteness makes Kx a finite union of closed cells, hence compact, and the continuous injection

continuous inverse, so j −1 is continuous at j(x). As x was arbitrary, j is a topological embedding.

Definition ([49]). Let c : S 1 → S be a closed curve and p : S ′ → S a covering map. An elevation of
c to S ′ is the map Z → S ′ induced by the second projection, where Z is a connected component of

                            S 1 ×S S ′ = {(z, s′ ) ∈ S 1 × S ′ : c(z) = p(s′ )}.

The first projection Z → S 1 is a connected covering; compare [49, Definition 2.3].

Lemma 5.3. Let σ be a cell of C, and let P, Q ≤ Π be the stabilizers of the two coordinate cells of
j(σ) in Tα , Tβ . The diagonal coordinate map

                            ισ : Π −→ Π/P × Π/Q,                  γ 7−→ (γP, γQ),

extends to a topological embedding

                                        ισ : Π
                                        b    b ,→ Π/P
                                                  b   × Π/Q.
                                                        b

Distinct original diagonal cell orbits in the product have disjoint closures.


                                                         36
Proof. Under the coset identifications, the two coordinate cells of j(σ) correspond to the identity
cosets P and Q. By Proposition 5.1, the map j is Π-equivariant and injective on cells. Consequently,
for every γ ∈ Π,
                                γσ = σ ⇐⇒ γj(σ) = j(σ)
                                            ⇐⇒       (γP, γQ) = (P, Q)
                                            ⇐⇒       γ ∈ P ∩ Q.
Hence StabΠ (σ) = P ∩ Q. Since the action on the cells of C is free by Proposition 5.1,

                                       P ∩ Q = StabΠ (σ) = {1}.

In addition, the subgroups P, Q are finitely generated, so by Theorem 3.6(ii) we have

                                          P ∩ Q = P ∩ Q = 1.

It follows that the completed diagonal map is injective, and a continuous injection from the compact
space Π b to a Hausdorff space is a topological embedding.
    A general pair has the form (sP, rQ), and acting diagonally by s−1 we get

                                      s−1 · (sP, rQ) = (P, s−1 rQ).

Hence every diagonal orbit contains a pair with first coordinate P , so we may use representatives
(P, rQ) with r ∈ Π, and the orbit of (P, rQ) depends only on the double coset P rQ. Suppose the
closures of the orbits represented by r, s ∈ Π meet. Then for some γ, δ ∈ Π
                                                                          b we have

                                        (γP , γrQ) = (δP , δsQ).

Put p = δ −1 γ. Then the first coordinate shows p ∈ P , and the second shows s−1 pr ∈ Q, so s ∈ P r Q.
Since the map P × Q → Π,    b (p, q) 7→ prq, has compact image and the image of the dense subset
P × Q is P rQ, we have P r Q = P rQ. Because s is discrete and P rQ is separable, s ∈ P rQ, so the
original orbits were equal.

Proposition 5.4. The Π-equivariant cellular embedding j : C(α, β) ,→ Tα × Tβ of the dual square
complex extends to an embedding of its profinite completion. The extended maps on vertices, edges,
and squares are continuous and commute with the structure maps. Its square space is the set of
products e × f with e ⋔ f . Every profinite edge and every profinite vertex of Cb is a face of a profinite
square.

Proof. Step 1. For each original cell orbit, Lemma 5.3 extends its coordinate map to a topological
embedding of a copy of Π, b and separates the closures of distinct cell orbits. We now treat
the occurrence spaces. Let Vξ , Eξ denote the completed vertex and edge spaces of Tξ and set
Jξ = Eξ × {0, 1}, for ξ ∈ {α, β}, as in Definition 1.7. The coordinate map sends endpoint
occurrences into
                                       (Jα × Vβ ) ⊔ (Vα × Jβ )
and side occurrences into
                                        (Jα × Eβ ) ⊔ (Eα × Jβ ).
Fix one component and one endpoint label in {0, 1}. A labelled endpoint occurrence is a pair
(e, r) ∈ Jξ = Eξ × {0, 1}. Its underlying edge is pξ (e, r) = e, and it is attached to the vertex

                                           qξ (e, r) = ∂ξ,r (e).

                                                    37
The label r = 0 refers to the initial endpoint and r = 1 to the terminal endpoint. The action of Π
                                                                                                 b
on the cut tree preserves the endpoint labels, so γ · (e, r) = (γe, r). Hence

                                   γ · (e, r) = (e, r)     ⇐⇒      γe = e,

and hence Stab(e, r) = Stab(e). We now describe the occurrence maps using the product coordinates
of j(C). All original coordinate pairs considered below represent cells of C under the embedding j.
    First consider an edge whose image has coordinates (e, v), where e is an edge of Tα and v is
a vertex of Tβ . Its endpoint occurrence with label r ∈ {0, 1} has coordinates ((e, r), v). The two
structure maps are
                                     p10 ((e, r), v) = (e, v),
                                       q10 ((e, r), v) = (∂α,r (e), v).
For an edge with coordinates (v, f ), where v is a vertex of Tα and f is an edge of Tβ , we have

                                      p10 (v, (f, r)) = (v, f ),
                                      q10 (v, (f, r)) = (v, ∂β,r (f )).

    Now consider a square with coordinates (e, f ), representing the product of an edge e of Tα and
an edge f of Tβ . The occurrence ((e, r), f ) specifies the side obtained by fixing the Tα -coordinate at
∂α,r (e). Therefore
                                    p21 ((e, r), f ) = (e, f ),
                                       q21 ((e, r), f ) = (∂α,r (e), f ).
Similarly, the occurrence (e, (f, r)) specifies the side obtained by fixing the Tβ -coordinate and in
this case we have
                                      p21 (e, (f, r)) = (e, f ),
                                       q21 (e, (f, r)) = (e, ∂β,r (f )).
The two choices of coordinate and the two choices of r account for all four side positions. Now fix
one of the two subsets in the disjoint union defining the occurrence space, and fix a label r ∈ {0, 1}.
With these choices, forgetting the label is a bijection between the occurrences coming from C and
the images of its edges or squares. For example, ((e, r), v) 7−→ (e, v) has inverse (e, v) 7→ ((e, r), v),
and ((e, r), f ) 7−→ (e, f ) has inverse (e, f ) 7→ ((e, r), f ). The correspondences with the label in the
second coordinate have the same property.
    Since the action preserves the labels, γ · (e, r) = (γe, r), all these bijections are Π-equivariant,
and they identify each occurrence orbit with the orbit of the edge or square to which it belongs.
    Since StabΠ (e, r) = StabΠ (e), the two coordinate stabilizers of an endpoint occurrence ((e, r), v)
are StabΠ (e) and StabΠ (v), and those of a side occurrence ((e, r), f ) are StabΠ (e) and StabΠ (f ).
Hence they are the coordinate stabilizers P, Q of the corresponding edge or square, and the same
holds when the label is in the second coordinate.
    By Lemma 5.3, the coordinate map on each such cell orbit extends to an embedding of a copy of
Π.
b Inserting the fixed label is continuous and has the continuous inverse that forgets it, because the
labels carry the discrete topology. Thus the coordinate map on each occurrence orbit also extends
to an embedding of a copy of Π.    b
    Hence within the chosen subset and with the label fixed, distinct occurrence orbits correspond
to distinct cell orbits, and by Lemma 5.3, their closures are also disjoint. Each profinite occurrence
space is the disjoint union of the two closed and open subsets above, within each of which the parts
labelled 0 and 1 are again disjoint and closed and open, and an orbit contained in one of these


                                                      38
subsets has its closure in the same subset. So orbit closures lying in different subsets or having
different labels cannot meet, and all distinct occurrence orbit closures are disjoint.

Homological crossing and independent unit powers
In this section we use the Boggi–Zalesskii criterion for finite covers; the realization argument only
requires the vanishing of algebraic intersection numbers. Most of the results and their proofs are
due to Boggi and Zalesskii [4, 5]. We state them in the form needed for our constructions and give
the proofs.
    For arbitrary nontrivial c, d ∈ Π, the notation i([c], [d]) = 0 means that their free homotopy
classes admit representatives with disjoint images. The two representatives are chosen independently,
including when the classes coincide. This vanishing condition is unchanged when either element is
replaced by a nonzero integral power.
Theorem 5.5. Let S be a closed orientable surface of genus at least two and Π = π1 (S). For
L ⊴f Π, let SL be the associated cover space and let VL (c) ≤ H1 (SL , Z) be the submodule generated
by the homology classes of all connected elevations of the closed curve represented by c ∈ Π \ {1}.
For c, d ∈ Π \ {1},

           i([c], [d]) = 0   ⇐⇒    VL (c) ⊥ VL (d) for every L in a cofinal normal family,

where ⊥ refers to the algebraic intersection form of the covering surface.
Proof. This is essentially the result of [5], with the class of all finite groups and with no punctures.
Their result applies to any cofinal family of normal finite index subgroups. In particular, the
standard characteristic family is admissible. Since algebraic intersection numbers are integers, the
criterion may equally be applied after extension of scalars to Qp , which is how it is used below.

   For a finite index normal subgroup L ⊴f Π, we identify L    b with its open closure in Π.b For a
prime p, we write Zp for the ring of p-adic integers and Qp for its field of fractions. We set

                  H1 (L, Zp ) = H1 (L, Z) ⊗Z Zp ,        H1 (L, Qp ) = H1 (L, Z) ⊗Z Qp .

Because L is finitely generated and its abelianization is free abelian, the maximal pro-p quotient of
the topological abelianization of L
                                  b is H1 (L, Zp ). We denote the resulting continuous map by

                                               b −→ H1 (L, Zp ).
                                       abL,p : L

For x ∈ Π,
        b we let nL (x) denote the order of the image of x in the finite quotient Π/
                                                                                  b Lb∼
                                                                                      = Π/L. Then

profinite simple closed curves appears in [4]. We give the proof for arbitrary profinite elements.

    Assume first that x ∈ Π. Since a connected elevation of the oriented closed curve represented
by x to the normal cover SL → S is determined by a double coset Lg⟨x⟩ ∈ L\Π/⟨x⟩, its covering
degree over the original curve is the order of the image of x in Π/L, namely nL (x). If a lift of the
initial point is chosen in the coset Lg then the corresponding oriented closed elevation represents
gxnL (x) g −1 ∈ L. Conversely, every connected elevation is obtained in this way. So the integral
homology classes of the discrete elements occurring in (5.3) are precisely the classes of all connected
elevations. Their Z-span is VL (x), and extension of scalars proves (i).

                                     hy nL (y) h−1 = (hz)xnL (x) (hz)−1 .

is the p-component of λ, and multiplication by λp is an invertible scalar on H1 (L, Qp ), so the span

   The next lemma is established for automorphisms in the proof of [5]. We give a cohomological
proof in the form needed for isomorphisms between two profinite surface groups.

Lemma 5.7. Let Li be closed orientable surface groups and let
                                                          ∼

For every prime p, the map induced by continuous abelianization,
                                                          ∼
