is a symplectic similitude for the alternating algebraic intersection forms ⟨ , ⟩i on H1 (Li , Zp ). Thus
there is εp ∈ Z×
               p such that

(5.4)                        ⟨ϑ∗ u, ϑ∗ v⟩2 = εp ⟨u, v⟩1       (u, v ∈ H1 (L1 , Zp )).




                                                       42
Proof. If one surface has genus zero, its group and its profinite completion are trivial. The other
surface also has genus zero, since a positive-genus surface group has a nontrivial finite abelian
quotient. Both first homology groups vanish, and we may take εp = 1. We therefore assume that
both surfaces have positive genus. We fix r ≥ 1 and set Rr = Z/pr Z. Since surface groups are good
[3], restriction from continuous cochains identifies the pullback induced by ϑ with an isomorphism
of graded rings
                                                        ∼
                                                        =
                                   ϑ∗r : H ∗ (L2 , Rr ) −
                                                        → H ∗ (L1 , Rr ).
Let Si be a closed oriented surface with π1 (Si ) = Li , and let ωi ∈ H 2 (Si , Rr ) = H 2 (Li , Rr ) be the
reductions of integral orientation classes normalized by ⟨ωi , [Si ]⟩ = 1, where [Si ] ∈ H2 (Si , Rr ) is the
fundamental class. Since the groups in degree two are free of rank one over Rr , there is a unique
δr ∈ Rr× such that
                                            ϑ∗r (ω2 ) = δr ω1 .
For a, b ∈ H 1 (S2 , Rr ), multiplicativity and evaluation on the fundamental classes give

                                  ϑ∗r (a) ⌣ ϑ∗r (b), [S1 ] = δr ⟨a ⌣ b, [S2 ]⟩.

The first cohomology groups have the same rank, so the surfaces have a common genus g. Now
choose integral symplectic bases for their first homology groups and reduce these bases modulo pr .
Then in these bases the intersection matrix is
                                                        !
                                              0 Ig
                                    J=             ,              J −1 = −J.
                                             −Ig 0

The evaluation-dual cohomology bases have cup-product matrix J as well. Let Mr be the matrix of
the homology map dual to ϑ∗r , with columns giving the images of the chosen basis of H1 (S1 , Rr ) so
the pullback matrix is MrT , and the evaluated cup-product identity reads Mr JMrT = δr J. Since
both Mr and δr are invertible, taking inverses and using J −1 = −J we get

                                Mr−T JMr−1 = δr−1 J,             MrT JMr = δr J.

Thus the homology map has the same multiplier δr , also for p = 2, since nothing here requires
division by 2. The coefficient reductions Rr+1 → Rr commute with ϑ∗ and preserve the chosen
integral orientation classes, so δr+1 mod pr = δr . We set εp = limr δr ∈ Z× p . Passing to the inverse
                                                                  ←−
limit in the last matrix identity yields (5.4). Finally, naturality of the evaluation pairing identifies
the dual homology map with the map induced by ϑ on maximal pro-p abelian quotients, hence with
the map ϑ∗ above.

                     θL,∗ WL1 ,p (c1 ) = WL2 ,p (c2 ),        θL,∗ WL1 ,p (d1 ) = WL2 ,p (d2 ),

the conjugators being irrelevant by conjugacy invariance and the unit powers by Lemma 5.6(ii).
   By this isomorphism and Lemma 5.6(i), we obtain, at every standard characteristic level,

                            VL1 (c1 ) ⊥ VL1 (d1 )        ⇐⇒     VL2 (c2 ) ⊥ VL2 (d2 ),

where one may test the equality after tensoring with Qp because algebraic intersection is integer
valued. Since the standard families of characteristic subgroups are cofinal, applying Theorem 5.5 on
both sides proves the claimed equivalence.

Corollary 5.10. Under the conditions of Proposition 5.9, put Ci = C(αi , βi ). The product map
Fα × Fβ , together with its induced maps on endpoint and side occurrences, restricts to a θ-equivariant
isomorphism of profinite cubical residual complexes
                                                      ∼
                                                      =
                                           F : I(C
                                               b 1) −
                                                    → I(C
                                                      b 2 ).

Proof. By Proposition 5.4, the completed coordinate embedding identifies I(C
                                                                         b i ) with a subdiagram
of
                                          I(Tbαi × Tbβi )
which is closed in each of the five profinite component spaces. The subdiagram consists of the
crossing squares and all their side edges, vertices, endpoint occurrences and side occurrences, with
the restricted structure maps. By Proposition 5.9, Fα × Fβ maps the completed square space of C1
bijectively onto that of C2 .
      Each completed square has four side occurrences in Ib21 (Ci ), and by the definition of face positions
in Definition 1.4, the product tree map takes these four positions bijectively to the side positions of
the corresponding square in Cb2 . It therefore induces a bijection on the completed side occurrence
spaces commuting with p21 and q21 . By Proposition 5.4, the completed map qb21 is surjective. Then
commutation with q21 yields surjectivity on the completed edge spaces, while injectivity follows
from the fact that Fα × Fβ is a homeomorphism of the products of completed cut trees. So the
induced product map is a bijection from the completed edge space of Cb1 onto that of Cb2 .
      Denote by F0 and F1 the maps induced by Fα × Fβ on the vertex and edge spaces of the products
Tbαi × Tbβi , respectively. The previous paragraph shows that F1 restricts to a bijection between
the completed edge spaces of C1 and C2 . Each completed edge has two endpoint occurrences in
Ib10 (Ci ). The product tree map induces a bijection on endpoint occurrence spaces: the occurrence
of an endpoint v of an edge e is mapped to the occurrence of F0 (v) as an endpoint of F1 (e). This
induced map commutes with p10 and q10 . By Proposition 5.4, the completed map qb10 is surjective.
Hence commutation with q10 yields surjectivity on the completed vertex spaces, while injectivity
again follows from the product homeomorphism, so the induced product map is a bijection from the
completed vertex space of Cb1 onto that of Cb2 . Consequently the restriction is bijective, in the order

                              Cb 2 ←− Ib21 (C) −→ Cb 1 ←− Ib10 (C) −→ Cb 0 ,

and commutes with all four structure maps. The inverse product map induces continuous inverse
maps on all five component spaces, commuting with the four structure maps. So the restriction is
the desired θ-equivariant isomorphism of profinite cubical residual complexes.

    Fix i ∈ {1, 2}. We first show that every cell stabilizer in Γi is trivial. Suppose that γ ∈ Γi
stabilizes an open edge e setwise. It then preserves e. By regularity, the characteristic map χe is
a homeomorphism onto e, so χ−1    e ◦ γ ◦ χe is a continuous self-map of a closed interval; it has a
fixed point, whose image under χe is fixed by γ. Freeness of the action implies γ = 1. Similarly, if
γ stabilizes an open square q setwise, the characteristic map χq identifies its restriction with the
continuous self-map χ−1q ◦ γ ◦ χq of the closed square, and Brouwer’s fixed point theorem produces a
fixed point. Again, freeness implies γ = 1, and StabΓi (σ) = {1} for every cell σ.
    By equivariance of the structure maps,

                         StabΓi (ι) ≤ StabΓi (p10 (ι)) = {1}        (ι ∈ I10 (Xi )),

and
                        StabΓi (κ) ≤ StabΓi (p21 (κ)) = {1}             (κ ∈ I21 (Xi )),
hence all occurrence stabilizers are trivial as well.
    Cocompactness and local finiteness give finitely many cell orbits: a compact subset whose
translates cover Xi meets only finitely many cells, and every cell orbit meets that subset. There
are also finitely many occurrence orbits, because each edge has two endpoint occurrences and each
square has four side occurrences.

Remark 5.12. The example after Definition 1.7 shows that the normalization in Theorem 5.11 cannot

original subset.

Proof of Theorem 2.7. Let [θ] ∈ Out(Π) b have a representative preserving the two marked conjugacy

Realization in every dimension from the completed full cellular residual complex
We now extend the realization argument to complexes of arbitrary dimension.

Proof of Theorem 2.3. The proof of Theorem 5.11 only uses the occurrence spaces of codimension
one faces and their two structure maps, and it goes through in every dimension; we indicate the
changes. Since Γi acts freely and Xi is regular, every cell stabilizer is trivial, by Brouwer’s fixed
point theorem applied to the characteristic map of the closed cell as in the proof of Theorem 5.11,
and hence every occurrence stabilizer is trivial as well. As in that proof, there are finitely many cell
and occurrence orbits, and each completed orbit is a free Γ b i -orbit meeting the original space in the
original orbit. Consequently, for every k, an occurrence in Ibk,k−1 (Xi ) is original as soon as its image
under pbk,k−1 or under qbk,k−1 is original.
    After a left translation we may assume that F sends some original vertex v1 of X1 to an original
vertex of X2 . Since the 1-skeleton is connected, ordinariness propagates along edge paths through
the endpoint occurrences, exactly as in the square case, so all original vertices, edges and endpoint
occurrences have original images. Suppose that all original cells of dimension less than k have
original images, and let σ be an original k-cell. Its boundary sphere contains a (k − 1)-cell τ , giving
an occurrence (σ, τ, ι) whose image under qbk,k−1 is the image of τ , which is original. Therefore
the occurrence has original image, and so does σ = pk,k−1 (σ, τ, ι). By induction on k, all original
cells and occurrences of X1 have original images, and the same argument applied to F −1 yields the
reverse inclusions. The identification of the dense subgroups through the action on v1 is exactly as
before.


6    Graph residual complexes
In this section we construct graph residual complexes and prove their realization theorem, which is
used in Section 12.

Characteristic quotients and directed edge residual complexes
                                   T
We keep the notation Km (G) = [G:L]≤m L from Definition 1.8, together with G,      b H, Inn(u), and
StabG (x). We write SΛ for the group of all permutations of a finite set Λ. For an action on a graph
or its completion, Lu denotes left multiplication by u on every component space. The notation
C (k) denotes the k-skeleton of a complex, whereas C k denotes its set of open k-cells. Directed
multigraphs, graph actions, and the reversal of an oriented edge use the definitions of [44]. Profinite
graphs and their quotient maps use [41].
    For the following definition, we use the standard directed graph structure, with edge reversal
as in [44] when present. Our profinite version uses separate profinite vertex and edge spaces,
corresponding to the closed and open edge case of [41].



                                                   51
Definition 6.1. A directed multigraph X consists of a vertex set V (X), a directed edge set A(X),
and initial and terminal maps
                                   sX , tX : A(X) −→ V (X).
Loops and parallel directed edges are allowed. Connectedness means connectedness of the underlying
undirected multigraph. Local finiteness means that each vertex is incident to finitely many directed
edges.
   When X comes from an undirected graph, each geometric edge has two distinct orientations.
We keep the reversal involution a 7→ ā, satisfying

                     ¯ = a,
                     ā           ā ̸= a,    sX (ā) = tX (a),      tX (ā) = sX (a).

The directed edge residual complex is
                                                                     
                                    D(X) = V (X), A(X), sX , tX ,

together with reversal when present.
   An isomorphism F : D(X) → D(Y ) consists of bijections

                              FV : V (X) → V (Y ),        FA : A(X) → A(Y )

such that
                              FV ◦ sX = sY ◦ FA ,         FV ◦ tX = tY ◦ FA ,
and, when reversal is present,
                                             FA (ā) = FA (a).
A profinite directed edge residual complex has profinite vertex and directed edge spaces and
continuous structure maps, including reversal when present. Its isomorphisms satisfy the same
identities, with FV and FA homeomorphisms.
    If a group N acts on X by automorphisms preserving these maps, its quotient residual complex
has component sets
                                    N \V (X),    N \A(X),
with
                      s(N a) = N sX (a),        t(N a) = N tX (a),       N a = N ā
when reversal is present. The induced reversal may have fixed points. If N a = N ā, then

                                        s(N a) = s(N ā) = t(N a),

so this directed edge is a loop in the quotient. Different directed edge orbits stay different, even
when their initial and terminal vertex orbits agree.

Definition 6.2. Let G ↷ X by directed graph automorphisms. A finite distinguished family is a
finite label set Λ and a family S = (Zλ )λ∈Λ of G-invariant subgraphs. Thus V (Zλ ) ⊆ V (X) and
A(Zλ ) ⊆ A(X) are invariant, the endpoint maps send A(Zλ ) into V (Zλ ), and reversal preserves
A(Zλ ) when present. An isomorphism maps a family (Z1,λ ) to a family (Z2,λ ) according to σ ∈ SΛ
if it sends Z1,λ onto Z2,σ(λ) for every λ. The distinguished family may be empty.




                                                     52
Definition 6.3. Let G be finitely generated and residually finite, acting cocompactly on a locally
finite directed multigraph X with finite vertex stabilizers. We define
                                                                 
                                   D
                                   b G (X) = lim D Km (G)\X .
                                             ←−

The inverse limit is taken on vertices, directed edges, both endpoint maps, and reversal when present.

Compatible selection at characteristic finite quotients
Lemma 6.6. Let k ≥ j. Every isomorphism (6.2) at level mk descends uniquely to a Φmj -equivariant
isomorphism at level mj . The descended map has the same permutation of the distinguished labels.
Proof. We set Ni,k = Kmk (Gi ) and Ni,j = Kmj (Gi ). Then Ni,k ≤ Ni,j , and the complex at level
mj is the quotient of the complex at level mk by Ni,j /Ni,k . Two vertices or directed edges at
level mk with the same image at level mj differ by an element of N1,j /N1,k . Equivariance and
Lemma 3.1 imply that their images differ by an element of N2,j /N2,k . The map therefore descends
and commutes with endpoints and reversal. The inverse descends in the same way, so the descended
map is bijective. The quotient images of the distinguished subgraphs are permuted by the same
permutation. Uniqueness follows from surjectivity of the quotient projections.

Theorem 6.7. Under Definition 6.5, permitted isomorphisms of finite quotients Ej can be chosen
at all selected levels so that
                                rk,j (Ek ) = Ej   (k ≥ j),
where rk,j is the descent map of Lemma 6.6. Their inverse limit is a Φ-equivariant isomorphism
                                                       ∼

(6.3)                                F :D
                                        b G (X1 ) −
                                           1      →Db G (X2 ).
                                                       2


If distinguished subgraphs are present, the same permutation σ ∈ P occurs at every selected level
and in the inverse limit.
Proof. Let Ej be the set of all permitted Φmj -equivariant isomorphisms, including the permutation
coordinate. Both quotient complexes and the permitted permutation group are finite, so Ej is finite.
It is nonempty by assumption. Descent satisfies
                                  rℓ,j = rk,j ◦ rℓ,k       (ℓ ≥ k ≥ j).
We give each Ej the discrete topology. The product E = j≥1 Ej is compact. For k ≥ j, the
                                                                  Q

condition rk,j (Ek ) = Ej defines a closed subset of E . Any finite collection of these conditions
involves finitely many indices. We choose N at least as large as all of them, choose any EN ∈ EN ,
and set every required lower coordinate equal to its descent from EN . Transitivity satisfies all
the conditions in the finite collection. The closed conditions therefore have the finite intersection
property, and by compactness there is a compatible family. See also [40].
   The selected subgroups are cofinal because (mj ) is unbounded. Taking inverse limits yields (6.3),
and the compatible inverse maps give its inverse. The permutation coordinate is unchanged by
descent, so the selected family involves a single permutation.

Remark 6.8. The descent maps rk,j are not necessarily surjective, and the maps selected by
Theorem 6.7 may differ from the initial choices.

Realization of original graph actions and their outer automorphisms
Proof of Theorem 2.4. Under condition (a), Theorem 6.7 provides compatible maps at the chosen
levels, a common permutation σ ∈ P, and a Φ-equivariant inverse limit isomorphism F satisfying
condition (b). Under condition (b), we use the given map F . It is therefore enough to prove the
realization conclusion from condition (b).
    We choose v1 ∈ V (X1 ). The point F (v1 ) belongs to a completed orbit G  b 2 /V2 , where V2 is the
finite stabilizer of some v2 ∈ V (X2 ). We choose w ∈ G2 with wF (v1 ) = v2 , and set

Then F ′ is Φ′ -equivariant and F ′ (v1 ) = v2 .
   Fix a directed edge orbit of X2 and choose an original representative a0 . Choose original
representatives x0 , y0 of its initial and terminal vertex orbits, and set

                     L = StabG2 (a0 ),      V = StabG2 (x0 ),           W = StabG2 (y0 ).

All three stabilizers are finite. Since x0 represents the G2 -orbit containing sX2 (a0 ), and y0 represents
the orbit containing tX2 (a0 ), there exist c, d ∈ G2 such that

                                   sX2 (a0 ) = cx0 ,        tX2 (a0 ) = dy0 .

By equivariance of the completed endpoint maps,

                                   s(ga0 ) = gcx0 ,         t(ga0 ) = gdy0 ,

so in these coset coordinates

                             s(gL) = gcV,        t(gL) = gdW            (g ∈ G
                                                                             b 2 ).

    These formulas are well defined, since an element of L fixes a0 and hence both of its endpoints,
so that
                                   c−1 Lc ≤ V,     d−1 Ld ≤ W.
Hence, replacing g by gℓ with ℓ ∈ L changes neither endpoint coset:

                    gℓcV = gc(c−1 ℓc)V = gcV,           gℓdW = gd(d−1 ℓd)W = gdW.

If gcV = γV for some γ ∈ G2 , then
                                            g ∈ γV c−1 ⊆ G2 .
Hence the directed edge and its other endpoint are original. The argument for the terminal map is
identical. This is the local occurrence argument of Theorem 5.11, adapted to finite stabilizers.
      Starting at v1 , apply the argument at the initial or terminal vertex to every incident directed edge.
Its image and its other endpoint are original. Induction on distance in the connected underlying
graph yields F ′ (V (X1 )) ⊆ V (X2 ) and F ′ (A(X1 )) ⊆ A(X2 ), and the local argument applied to
(F ′ )−1 turns these inclusions into equalities. Because distinguished subgraphs are invariant unions
of orbits, their completions meet the original complex in their original vertices and directed edges.
The distinguished family claim follows.
      For γ ∈ G1 , by equivariance F ′ (γv1 ) = Φ′ (γ)v2 . This vertex is original, so Φ′ (γ)V2 = δV2 for
some δ ∈ G2 , and Φ′ (γ) ∈ δV2 ⊆ G2 . The inverse argument shows Φ′ (G1 ) = G2 . We set h = Φ′ |G1 .
Continuity and density imply Φ′ = h,   b and (2.2) follows with u = w−1 .
      Conversely, a map F as in condition (b) descends to every characteristic quotient: quotienting
each completed orbit by Km (Gi ) determines the orbit at level m, while Φ identifies the relevant
open normal subgroups by Lemma 3.1, and the descended maps commute with the endpoint maps,
reversal and the distinguished subgraphs. So condition (b) implies condition (a) as well.

Corollary 6.9. Under the remaining conditions of Theorem 2.4, suppose the actions are free on
vertices and directed edges. Then its realization conclusion holds, and every completed vertex and
directed edge orbit is a free profinite orbit.

Proof. The vertex and directed edge stabilizers are trivial. We apply Lemma 6.4 and Theorem 2.4.

Theorem 6.11. Under the action conditions of Theorem 2.4, the homomorphism (6.4) is an
isomorphism.

Proof. A continuous pair (Φ, F ) on the completed action descends to every characteristic quotient
by Lemma 3.1. By Theorem 2.4, a single diagonal left translation makes this pair restrict to a
discrete pair (h, F ′ |X ) preserving every distinguished label. Its completion is the translated profinite
pair because the maps agree on the dense original vertex and directed edge sets. So the map is
surjective.
    For injectivity, suppose a discrete pair (α, A) becomes diagonal: α      b = Inn(u) and A  b = Lu for
some u ∈ G. b We choose v ∈ V (X) and set V = StabG (v), a finite subgroup. As A(v) = uv is
original, uV = γV for some γ ∈ G, and u ∈ γV ⊆ G. Restricting both equalities to the original
subsets, we get α = Inn(u) and A = Lu . The discrete pair was already diagonal.

Realization from a graph
Definition 6.12. Let C be a connected finite simplicial complex. A finite connected subgraph
Y ⊆ C (1) is π1 -surjective if its inclusion induces a surjection π1 (Y ) ↠ π1 (C). We give Y the directed
edge definition with both orientations of every geometric edge.

Lemma 6.13. Every connected finite simplicial complex contains a π1 -surjective finite connected
subgraph. If p : Ce → C is the universal cover, G = π1 (C) is its deck group, and Y is such a subgraph,
then X = p−1 (Y ) is connected and locally finite. The action G ↷ X is free and cocompact and
G\X = Y .

Proof. The inclusion of the 1-skeleton C (1) induces a surjection on fundamental groups, since every
loop in C is homotopic to an edge path [18], so C (1) itself is a π1 -surjective finite connected subgraph.
Now let Y be any such subgraph. The components of p−1 (Y ) correspond to the cosets of the image
of π1 (Y ) in π1 (C), so X = p−1 (Y ) is connected precisely because the inclusion of Y is π1 -surjective.
It is locally finite because C is finite, and the deck group acts freely on C,
                                                                             e hence on X, with quotient
p(X) = Y ; in particular the action is cocompact.

Full simplex face residual complexes and PL realization
Definition 6.15. For a finite dimensional simplicial complex X, we use the full cellular residual
complex I• (X) of Definition 1.12. A simplex and one of its codimension one faces determine a
unique face position, so the occurrence spaces identify with

                        Ik,k−1 (X) = {(σ, τ ) ∈ X k × X k−1 : τ is a face of σ},

with pk,k−1 (σ, τ ) = σ and qk,k−1 (σ, τ ) = τ . This pair notation identifies the occurrence triples of
Definition 1.12. For any subgroup N acting cellularly on X, we keep

                                        I• (N \X) = N \I• (X)

on every cell and occurrence space. In particular, the occurrence space is N \Ik,k−1 (X). Distinct
occurrence orbits remain distinct even when their incident quotient cells coincide. The same rule
for orbits of face occurrences applies to regular CW complexes.
    A distinguished invariant subcomplex Zλ specifies the subsets Zλk ⊆ X k and Ik,k−1 (Zλ ) ⊆
Ik,k−1 (X) in every dimension. A map preserves a finite family of such subcomplexes if it preserves
all these subsets according to the same label permutation. Completions are the component-wise
characteristic inverse limits of Definition 1.12.

Proof of Theorem 2.5. The descent argument of Lemma 6.6 applies simultaneously to every cell
space and every codimension one occurrence space. At each selected level, the set of permitted
isomorphisms is finite and nonempty. The compactness argument of Theorem 6.7 therefore produces
compatible isomorphisms, with one permutation of the distinguished labels when present. Their
inverse limit is a Φ-equivariant isomorphism
                                                     ∼
                                                     =
                                        F : Ib• (X1 ) −
                                                      → Ib• (X2 ).

   Each Xi is a connected, locally finite, finite dimensional simplicial complex. Its deck group
Gi acts freely and cocompactly, and Gi is finitely generated because Ci is finite. It follows that
Theorem 2.3 applies and provides u ∈ Gb 2 and an isomorphism h : G1 → G2 such that

                                 Φ = Inn(u) ◦ h,
                                              b          F ′ = Lu−1 ◦ F

restricts to an h-equivariant isomorphism between the full original residual complexes.

                                                   57
    By iterating the codimension one face relations, F ′ preserves every face relation. In particular,
it sends the vertex set of each simplex onto the vertex set of its image. A simplex is determined by
its vertices, so these maps define a simplicial isomorphism X1 → X2 . Its affine extensions agree on
common faces. By equivariance we therefore have a simplicial, hence PL, homeomorphism
                                                 ∼
                                                 =
                                   C1 = G1 \X1 −
                                               → G2 \X2 = C2 .

Lifting based loops shows that its induced homomorphism on fundamental groups is h, up to the
choice of basepoint paths.
    Finally, each distinguished invariant subcomplex is a union of cell and occurrence orbits. Its
completion intersects the original residual complex in precisely those orbits. Consequently, F ′ and
the descended homeomorphism preserve the distinguished subcomplexes according to the selected
permutation.


7       Massey triple products
Let M be a compact oriented 3-manifold. We use the Thurston seminorm on H 1 (M, R) and, when
present, its fibered cones [47, 3]. For a closed hyperbolic M , or a compact core of a complete finite
volume hyperbolic 3-manifold, the Thurston seminorm is a norm.

Cohomological background
Definition. Let R be a commutative ring. A differential graded R-algebra A∗ is a graded unital
associative R-algebra with an R-linear differential d : Ak → Ak+1 satisfying d2 = 0 and d(xy) =
d(x)y + (−1)k xd(y) for x ∈ Ak . Its cohomology is H k (A) = ker(d : Ak → Ak+1 )/ im(d : Ak−1 → Ak ).
A morphism of differential graded R-algebras is a cochain map preserving the unit and multiplication.
Such a morphism is a quasi-isomorphism if it induces an isomorphism in every cohomological degree.
For a, b, c ∈ H 1 (A) with ab = bc = 0, we define

            ⟨a, b, c⟩A = {[AV + U C] : [A] = a, [B] = b, [C] = c, dU = AB, dV = BC},

where A, B, C are cocycles of degree one and U, V ∈ A1 . Juxtaposition denotes the algebra product,
written ⌣ for cup products. This is the triple Massey product with the above sign definition. Its
indeterminacy is aH 1 (A) + H 1 (A)c, and the word complete refers to the whole set of classes in this
definition. See [29, Definition 1.2, Theorem 1.5 and Proposition 2.3].

    Let Φ : π1 M1 → π1 M2 be an isomorphism of the completions of orientable connected finite
            \        \
volume hyperbolic 3-manifold groups. We begin with Liu’s regularity theorem [25, Theorems 1.2–1.3
and Corollary 6.2], which gives
                                                        ∼
                                                        =
(7.1)              Φ∗ = µ(F ⊗ Z),
                              b         F : H 1 (M2 , Z) −
                                                         → H 1 (M1 , Z),     b ×.
                                                                           µ∈Z

The integral map F and its inverse preserve the Thurston norm and fibered classes, and corresponding
fiber kernels have corresponding closures. From here on in this section all manifolds are connected
and closed.

Lemma 7.1. Let R be a commutative ring and let

                                           F : A∗ −→ B ∗


                                                 58
be a unital morphism of differential graded R-algebras that induces an isomorphism on cohomology.
Let a, b, c ∈ H 1 (A) satisfy a ⌣ b = b ⌣ c = 0. Then
                                              
(7.2)                            F∗ ⟨a, b, c⟩A = ⟨F∗ a, F∗ b, F∗ c⟩B .

Moreover, F∗ maps the full indeterminacy subgroup

                                     a ⌣ H 1 (A) + H 1 (A) ⌣ c

isomorphically onto
                                  F∗ a ⌣ H 1 (B) + H 1 (B) ⌣ F∗ c.
The same conclusion holds for the cohomology isomorphism obtained from a finite sequence of unital
multiplicative quasi-isomorphisms pointing in either direction.

Proof. Choose cocycles A, B, C representing a, b, c and cochains U, V with

                                 dU = A ⌣ B,           dV = B ⌣ C.

With the definition above in degree one, the corresponding Massey cocycle is A ⌣ V + U ⌣ C. Since
F commutes with the differential and product, the five cochains F (A), F (B), F (C), F (U ), F (V )
form a defining system in B ∗ , and

                      F (A ⌣ V + U ⌣ C) = F (A) ⌣ F (V ) + F (U ) ⌣ F (C).

Thus the left side of (7.2) is contained in the right side.
    We check directly that the full indeterminacy is the subgroup in the statement. Replacing U
by U + Z for a cocycle of degree one Z changes the Massey class by [Z] ⌣ c, and replacing V by
V + Z ′ changes it by a ⌣ [Z ′ ]. Conversely, every element of a ⌣ H 1 (A) + H 1 (A) ⌣ c is obtained
in this way. We also check changes of representatives. For s ∈ A0 , replacing A by A + ds and U
by U + sB changes the Massey cocycle by d(sV ). Replacing B by B + ds, U by U − As, and V
by V + sC leaves it unchanged. Replacing C by C + ds and V by V − Bs changes it by −d(U s).
Each replacement preserves the defining equations, so changing representatives does not change the
coset, and a nonempty complete triple Massey product is exactly one coset of its full indeterminacy
subgroup, as in [29, Proposition 2.3].
    The ring isomorphism F∗ maps the first indeterminacy subgroup isomorphically onto the second.
Let mA be the class of the chosen defining system. The image F∗ (⟨a, b, c⟩A ) is therefore the coset

                            F∗ (mA ) + F∗ a ⌣ H 1 (B) + H 1 (B) ⌣ F∗ c.

The complete product in B contains F∗ (mA ) and is a coset of the same subgroup. The two cosets
are equal, which proves (7.2). For a leftward quasi-isomorphism, apply the result to its induced
cohomology isomorphism and then invert that isomorphism. Composition proves the statement for
a finite sequence of these maps.

   Our next lemma compares finite-coefficient cohomology and complete triple Massey products
through the goodness of 3-manifold groups.
                                                                                         ∼
                                                                                         =
                                                                                    1P −
Lemma 7.2. Let P, Q be connected closed orientable aspherical 3-manifolds, let Ψ : πd  → πd
                                                                                          1 Q be
an isomorphism, and set Rn = Z/nZ for n ≥ 1. Then there are isomorphisms
                                                       ∼
                                                       =
                                  Ψ∗n : H ∗ (Q, Rn ) −
                                                     → H ∗ (P, Rn ),

                                                  59
natural under change of coefficients, which preserve cup products and complete triple Massey products
of classes of degree one, including their full indeterminacy. In addition, if ωP ∈ H 3 (P, Z) and
ωQ ∈ H 3 (Q, Z) are orientation classes evaluating to one, then

                           Ψ∗n (ωQ mod n) = δn (ωP mod n),                                  b ×.
                                                                               (δn )n = δ ∈ Z

Proof. Choose a basepoint in P and set G = π1 (P ). Both G and G    b act trivially on the coefficient
                    ∗
module Rn . Let Cb (G, Rn ) denote the normalized bar complex for group cohomology [7]. The
continuous complex Ccts∗ (G,
                          b Rn ) is defined by the same formulas, with continuous functions on (G) b k
and the discrete topology on Rn [40]. The usual normalization maps and homotopies are finite sums
of composites of face and degeneracy operators in each degree. They also preserve continuity, so the
normalized continuous complex computes continuous cohomology. In both complexes the product is

         (f ⌣ g)(g1 , . . . , gp+q ) = f (g1 , . . . , gp )g(gp+1 , . . . , gp+q )    (deg f = p, deg g = q).

Restriction along G → G   b therefore preserves the unit, differential, and product. As compact 3-
manifold groups are good [3], this restriction induces an isomorphism on cohomology with coefficients
in Rn . It is consequently a unital multiplicative quasi-isomorphism.
     Let BG be the simplicial bar construction with k-simplices Gk , and let |BG| be its geometric
realization [7]. For a simplicial set K, write N ∗ (K, Rn ) for the normalized simplicial cochain
complex, whose degree-q cochains are functions Kq → Rn vanishing on degenerate simplices. We
equip this complex with the usual simplicial coboundary and the Alexander–Whitney cup product,
making it a differential graded Rn -algebra. Write Sing X for the singular simplicial set of a space X.
Because P is aspherical and has the homotopy type of a CW complex, there is a based classifying
homotopy equivalence cP : P → |BG| inducing the identity of G under the canonical identification
π1 (|BG|) = G. Recall that for normalized cochains, the Alexander–Whitney product is used [7].
For a simplex σ of dimension p + q, this product evaluates f on the face with vertices 0, . . . , p and g
on the face with vertices p, . . . , p + q, and it is natural under simplicial maps and agrees with the
product under Cb∗ (G, Rn ) ∼= N ∗ (BG, Rn ). Altogether we obtain the following sequence of unital
multiplicative quasi-isomorphisms:
                                                                             cP                    ∗
(7.3)    ∗
        Ccts  b Rn ) −→ C ∗ (G, Rn ) ∼
             (G,                     = N ∗ (BG, Rn ) ←− N ∗ (Sing |BG|, Rn ) −→ N ∗ (Sing P, Rn ).
                         b

The middle arrow is induced by the canonical simplicial map BG → Sing |BG|. This map is a weak
equivalence and induces a homology isomorphism on normalized integral chains. Its mapping cone
is an acyclic complex of free abelian groups. Its cycles are free, so the short exact sequences in
that cone split and the cone is contractible. Applying Hom(−, Rn ) therefore shows that the middle
arrow is a quasi-isomorphism for every n. The last arrow is a quasi-isomorphism because cP is a
homotopy equivalence. All arrows obviously preserve the Alexander–Whitney product.
    Now we do exactly the same for Q and then pull back continuous cochains by using Ψ, thereby
obtaining Ψ∗n . As above, simplicial maps and classifying maps are chosen independently of n,
and restriction of group cochains also commutes with coefficient reduction. Hence their induced
cohomology isomorphisms and inverses commute with Rm → Rn whenever n | m. In degree one a
class corresponds under these identifications to its homomorphism to Rn , so Ψ∗n is precomposition
with Ψ. They also commute with restriction to finite index subgroups: for a connected finite cover
p : P ′ → P and the corresponding inclusion ι : G′ ,→ G, the two classifying maps

                                        cP ◦ p,         |Bι| ◦ cP ′ : P ′ −→ |BG|


                                                              60
induce the same homomorphism on fundamental groups, after compatible choices of basepoints.
As |BG| is a K(G, 1), these maps are homotopic and induce the same pullback on cohomology.
Restriction of bar and continuous cochains commutes with the subgroup inclusions. The same
argument applies to Q and its covers.
    Naturality of cup products follows from multiplicativity. Naturality of triple Massey products
as sets, including the full indeterminacy, follows from Lemma 7.1 applied to the two sequences of
identification maps.
    Since P and Q are connected closed orientable 3-manifolds,

                                H 3 (P, Rn ) ∼
                                             = Rn ,            H 3 (Q, Rn ) ∼
                                                                            = Rn .

Thus Ψ∗n multiplies the chosen orientation generator by a unique unit δn ∈ Rn× . If n | m, naturality
under Rm → Rn shows δm mod n = δn . It follows that (δn )n defines an element δ ∈ limn Rn = Z.     b
                                                                                        ←−
Every component is a unit, so δ ∈ Zb ×.

    Finally, the definition of triple Massey products gives

                                 ⟨λa, λb, λc⟩ = λ3 ⟨a, b, c⟩            (λ ∈ Rn× ).

Indeed, multiplying the three cocycle representatives by λ and the two cochains solving the defining
equations by λ2 proves one inclusion, and the same construction with λ−1 proves the reverse
inclusion.

Lemma 7.3. There exists a connected closed oriented 3-manifold Y with integral classes x1 , x2 , x3 , u, v
of degree one and m ∈ ⟨u, u, v⟩ such that

(7.4)               ⟨x1 x2 x3 , [Y ]⟩ = 1,        ⟨mv, [Y ]⟩ = 1,           u2 = uv = v 2 = 0.

Proof. Let H = Z3 with multiplication

and let N = H\H(R) be the compact Heisenberg nilmanifold, where H(R) is R3 with the same
multiplication. Its universal cover H(R) is contractible, so N is a K(H, 1). We identify integral
group cohomology with H ∗ (N, Z) using the group, simplicial, and singular identifications in (7.3),
with G = H, P = N , and coefficient ring Z, ignoring the continuous cochain complex. The
same contracting homotopy on the integral chain mapping cone proves that these identifications
are quasi-isomorphisms over Z. Their multiplicativity and Lemma 7.1 identify cup products and
complete triple Massey products. On normalized integral group cochains, we define

                                      M (ex , ez ) − M (ez , ex ) = −1.

Finally, choose the orientation of N to make this value 1.
    Now set Y = T 3 #N , where T 3 = (R/Z)3 . Let pT : Y → T 3 and pN : Y → N be obtained by
collapsing the separating sphere of the connected sum and then collapsing the other summand to a
point. With the orientations defining the oriented connected sum,

                                 (pT )∗ [Y ] = [T 3 ],        (pN )∗ [Y ] = [N ],

so both maps have degree one. Pulling back the three standard classes of T 3 and the classes a, b, [M ]
along these two pinch maps yields (7.4), since products of classes of positive degree coming from
different summands vanish.

8    Periodic orbits
Recall that a homeomorphism f of a closed surface is called pseudo-Anosov if there are two transverse
measured singular foliations (F1 , µ1 ) and (F2 , µ2 ) and a number λ > 1 such that f preserves each
foliation and f ∗ µ1 = λµ1 , f ∗ µ2 = λ−1 µ2 . The foliations have a common finite singular set, with at
least three prongs at each singular point. The measures are transverse measures invariant under
homotopies along leaves, see [12].
    Let f : S → S be an orientation preserving pseudo-Anosov homeomorphism of a closed orientable
surface of genus at least two, let Mf be its mapping torus and φs its suspension flow. The first return
map to S × {0} is f . Denote by Gf = π1 (Mf ) and let χ : Gf → Z be induced by the projection
Mf → R/Z. We choose t with suspension coordinate χ(t) = 1. If Π = ker χ, then a = conjt |Π
gives Gf = Π ⋊a ⟨t⟩, and [a] = [f∗−1 ]. The fixed point indices and return maps below concern the
forward map f , whereas the calculations with the stable letter in Section 10 use a. Both mapping

                                                       64
classes are pseudo-Anosov. Thurston’s hyperbolization theorem makes Mf closed hyperbolic [38],
so Theorem 3.7 applies. Given an orbit O of least return period d | m, we let aO be the element
represented by one positive traversal, up to conjugacy, and set
                                    m/d
                          bO,m = aO ,
                                   X
                        wm (O) =         ind(f m , x) = d ind(f m , x0 )   (x0 ∈ O).
                                   x∈O

Let Om (f ) be the finite collection of orbits of period dividing m. At an isolated fixed point x, the
local index ind(f m , x) is the degree of z 7→ (z − f m (z))/∥z − f m (z)∥ on the boundary of a sufficiently
small oriented coordinate disk about x, and the coefficient wm (O) is the sum of the local indices
over the whole orbit.
    Two points of Fix(f m ) are in the same fixed point class if there is a path between them that is
homotopic, relative to its endpoints, to its image under f m . The map f permutes these classes. An
orbit of this permutation is a periodic Nielsen class at return time m. We use the fixed point and
periodic orbit definitions of [22].

Lemma 8.1. Given the suspension flow of f : S → S on its mapping torus Mf , we have wm (O) ̸= 0
for every O ∈ Om (f ). Distinct orbits give distinct conjugacy classes [bO,m ] in Gf . Every prime
positive (forward traversal) suspension orbit determines, by one positive traversal, an element that
is not a proper power in Gf and has no proper integer root in G bf .

Proof. For a pseudo-Anosov iterate, every nonempty fixed point class is a singleton [22, Lemma 3.1].
Also at a fixed point with k stable prongs, the index is 1 − k when the prongs are fixed and is 1
otherwise. At a regular point we have k = 2, and at a singular point k ≥ 3. So every local index is
nonzero. Periodic Nielsen classes are the unions of these singleton point classes under f , so they are
the individual geometric periodic orbits. The suspension correspondence between periodic Nielsen
classes and conjugacy classes [22] then shows that the classes [bO,m ] are distinct.
    We next prove the absence of proper roots in the group. Identify the universal cover of the
mapping torus with Se × R using the lifted suspension. Collapsing each lifted flow line gives the orbit
plane Se ∼= R2 . Let θe : M
                          ff → R be the lift of the fibration Mf → R/Z; every deck transformation
g ∈ Gf satisfies θ(gz)
                  e        e + χ(g). Since deck transformations commute with the lifted suspension
                         = θ(z)
                                                     ff ∼
                                                                                                      
flow by uniqueness of lifting, in the coordinates M      = Se × R we have g(x, s) = ḡ(x), s + χ(g) ,
where ḡ is the induced homeomorphism of the orbit plane. Because the return map preserves the
orientation of S, each element of Gf induces an orientation preserving homeomorphism of the orbit
plane. Let b be represented by one positive traversal of a prime trajectory, and suppose that b = g r
for some r ≥ 2. The element b fixes the point of the orbit plane represented by a lift of its closed
trajectory. Thus g r has a fixed point. By the Brouwer plane translation theorem, an orientation
preserving fixed point free homeomorphism of R2 has no periodic point [13]. Therefore g has a fixed
point in the orbit plane. It preserves a lifted flow line and translates it in the positive direction,
because rχ(g) = χ(b) > 0, so g represents a positive closed trajectory. Its r-fold return element is
g r = b, so this trajectory and the one represented by b determine the same periodic Nielsen class.
Each such class consists of one geometric periodic orbit, so these trajectories are the same orbit. But
χ(g) = χ(b)/r > 0 would give that orbit a shorter positive return time. This contradicts primeness,
and b is not a proper power in Gf .
    Now for an element b that is not a proper power, the discrete centralizer is ⟨b⟩. The profinite
centralizer comparison in Theorem 3.7 implies

                                              CGb (b) = ⟨b⟩ ∼
                                                            = Z.

9    Fibered classes
Let G be a finite directed graph with directed edge set A(G) and endpoint maps s, t. We define its
two sided edge shift by

              Σ = {(ej )j∈Z ∈ A(G)Z : t(ej ) = s(ej+1 ) for all j},        σ((ej )j ) = (ej+1 )j ,

with the subspace topology from the product of finite discrete sets. The graph is strongly connected
if every ordered pair of vertices is joined by a directed path. An admissible word is a finite nonempty
directed edge path. It is closed if its last terminal vertex equals its first initial vertex. Repeating
a closed word W of length k defines a point ωW ∈ Σ with σ k ωW = ωW . For a continuous map
π : Σ → S with πσ = f π, a periodic realization of W means the orbit of xW = π(ωW ), traversed for
                              (k)                                                   (k)
k return times. We write cW for this oriented closed suspension curve and [cW ] ∈ H1 (Mf , Z) for
its homology class. See [37].

Lemma 9.1. Let S be a connected closed orientable surface of genus at least two, and let f : S → S
be an orientation preserving pseudo-Anosov homeomorphism. Then there are a finite strongly
connected directed graph G, its two sided edge shift (Σ, σ), and a continuous surjective map with
finite fibers
                                           π : Σ −→ S
such that πσ = f π. In addition, every closed admissible word has a periodic realization. Moreover,
if f m (x) = x, then there are ω ∈ π −1 (x) and r ≥ 1 with σ mr (ω) = ω.

Proof. Choose a finite Markov partition R1 , . . . , Rq by good rectangles, that is, closed rectangles
which are embedded and homeomorphic to closed disks, with sides in stable and unstable leaves, and
which satisfy the one-crossing condition of [11]. For each pair (i, j), put Uij = int Ri ∩ f −1 (int Rj ),
the set of points in int Ri whose image lies in int Rj . If Uij is nonempty, the one-crossing condition
makes it connected. The Markov rectangle structure then shows that Qe = Uij is a closed topological
rectangle, hence a closed disk. We introduce one directed edge e : i → j for this transition and
associate the disk Qe to it. If Uij is empty, we introduce no such edge, even if Ri ∩ f −1 (Rj ) is
nonempty. Boundary-only intersections give no edge, and the rectangle transition matrix is binary.
Its positive power in [11, Lemma 1] makes G strongly connected. For an admissible edge sequence
ω = (ej )j∈Z , set ij = s(ej ). The nonempty cylinder rectangles

                                                   N
                                                   \
                                      CN (ω) =           f −j (int Rij )
                                                 j=−N

are nested and shrink to a point. Their intersection defines a continuous surjection π with πσ = f π
by [11].

                                                    70
     For the finiteness of the fibers π −1 (x) we follow [9, Lemmas 9–10 and Corollary 1]. At a point x,
the local stable and unstable prongs bound finitely many open sector germs, four at a regular point
and 2k at a k-prong singularity. Their images under every iterate are again sector germs. Each such
image also lies in the interior of a unique partition rectangle, since its boundary consists of foliation
arcs. So a sector determines at most one complete rectangle itinerary and so by the one-crossing
condition, at most one edge sequence. Representatives of the germ are taken small enough to avoid
the finitely many partition boundary arcs that do not pass through the point. Conversely, suppose
π(ω) = x. Then each CN (ω) contains x and has nonempty interior bounded by stable and unstable
arcs. So its interior contains an open sector germ at x. Note that the sets of such germs are finite,
nonempty, and nested as N increases. Hence a germ in their intersection has itinerary (ij )j∈Z , so
it determines ω. Therefore every name of x comes from a sector, and π −1 (x) is finite. This also
proves finiteness at boundaries and singularities.
     Repeating a closed admissible word produces a periodic sequence and hence a periodic realization.
If f m (x) = x, then both σ m and σ −m preserve the finite nonempty set π −1 (x). Thus σ m permutes
that set. Some positive power of the permutation fixes a name, which implies the final claim.

Lemma 9.2. Let Mf be the suspension of f . Fix the Markov rectangles R1 , . . . , Rq , the closed
rectangles Qe , the directed graph G, and the mapping π constructed in the proof of Lemma 9.1.
There are classes h(e) ∈ H1 (Mf , Z), one for each directed edge e of G, such that, for every closed
admissible word W = e1 · · · ek and every periodic realization traversed for exactly k return times, we
have
                                             k
                                      (k)   X
(9.1)                               [cW ] =     h(ej )         in H1 (Mf , Z).
                                            j=1

The formula remains valid when the realization meets a boundary of a Markov rectangle or a
singularity of the invariant foliations.

Proof. We use the rectangles and transition disks of Lemma 9.1. Fix a basepoint o ∈ S, a point
ri ∈ Ri for each i, and a path γi from o to ri . For e : i → j, choose ze ∈ Qe and paths αe from ri to
ze in Ri and βe from f (ze ) to rj in Rj . Write τz for the positively oriented unit suspension segment
from z to f (z). We define
                                  h(e) = [γi αe τze βe γj−1 ] ∈ H1 (Mf , Z).
Let W = e1 · · · ek be closed and let x1 , . . . , xk be its successive realization points, with xk+1 = x1
and f (xℓ ) = xℓ+1 . Since xℓ ∈ Qeℓ and Qeℓ is a disk, we can choose a path aℓ in Qeℓ from zeℓ to xℓ .
The map
                               Hℓ (u, t) = φt (aℓ (u))        ((u, t) ∈ [0, 1]2 )
deforms the chosen suspension segment into the realized one. Its endpoint paths remain in Rs(eℓ )
and Rt(eℓ ) . Next choose paths δℓ from rs(eℓ ) to xℓ in Rs(eℓ ) , with δk+1 = δ1 . Contractibility of both
                                                                                                   −1 −1
endpoint rectangles shows that the loop defining h(eℓ ) is based homotopic to γs(eℓ ) δℓ τxℓ δℓ+1     γt(eℓ ) .

                                      Aℓ = γs(eℓ ) δℓ ,         Ak+1 = A1 .

Then the based homotopies above give

                                      L1 · · · Lk ≃ A1 (τx1 · · · τxk )A−1
                                                                        1 ,

because each intervening path A−1

is the realized closed suspension curve c . Passing to the abelianization π1 (Mf , o)ab ∼

proves (9.1) in the full integral homology group, including torsion.
    The paths aℓ lie in the closed transition disks, and the comparisons of endpoint paths take place
in the closed contractible rectangles. Since the suspension homotopies are continuous at every point,
the argument also applies to realization points on partition boundaries or at singularities of the
invariant foliations.

Lemma 9.3. Let G be a finite strongly connected directed graph with an integer weight w(e) on
each directed edge. For W = e1 · · · ek , we set w(W ) = kj=1 w(ej ). If G has closed admissible words
                                                        P

of positive and negative total weight, then it has a closed admissible word of total weight zero.
Proof. Choose a vertex v. Let W+ and W− be closed words of positive and negative weight, based
at vertices v+ and v− . By strong connectivity there are directed paths from v to v+ and back,
and from v to v− and back. The connecting paths have fixed total weights. Since w(W+ ) > 0 and
w(W− ) < 0, repeating W+ sufficiently many times makes the resulting closed word based at v have
positive weight, and repeating W− sufficiently many times makes the resulting closed word based at
v have negative weight. We obtain closed words P+ and P− based at v with weights
                                w(P+ ) = p > 0,            w(P− ) = −q < 0.
Then the closed word P+q P−p has weight qp − pq = 0.

Lemma 9.4. Let S be a connected closed orientable surface of genus at least two, and let f : S → S
be an orientation preserving pseudo-Anosov homeomorphism. Equip its mapping torus M = Mf with
the suspension flow, with positive direction given by increasing time. Suppose that η ∈ H 1 (M, Z)
has opposite signs on two positive periodic orbits c+ , c− . Then the flow has a positive prime orbit
with representative b ∈ π1 (M ) such that η(b) = 0, and b is not a proper power in π1 (M ).
Proof. Apply Lemma 9.1 to periodic points on c+ and c− . After passing to positive multiples of
their return periods, we obtain periodic admissible words whose realizations traverse c+ and c− ,
respectively, r+ and r− times, where r+ , r− ≥ 1. Their homology classes are therefore r+ [c+ ] and
r− [c− ]. By additivity of η,
                     η(r+ [c+ ]) = r+ η([c+ ]) > 0,        η(r− [c− ]) = r− η([c− ]) < 0.
We regard η ∈ H 1 (M, Z) as the homomorphism H1 (M, Z) −→ Z, a 7−→ ⟨η, a⟩. For each directed
edge e, we define its integer weight by w(e) = ⟨η, h(e)⟩ where h(e) is from Lemma 9.2. By Lemma 9.3,
there is a closed admissible word of total weight zero, and its periodic realization has zero evaluation
under η by (9.1).
    Let b be the element represented by one positive traversal of the resulting prime trajectory. The
original realization traverses this prime trajectory a positive integer number of times. As Z has no
torsion, the zero evaluation under η forces η(b) = 0. Finally by Lemma 8.1, this element b is not a
proper power in the suspension group.

Theorem 9.5. Let M be a closed orientable hyperbolic 3-manifold and set G = π1 (M ). There is
a standard characteristic subgroup K = Km (G) such that the corresponding cover pK : MK → M
admits linearly independent primitive fibered classes
                                        χ0,K , χ1,K ∈ H 1 (MK , Z)
together with a positive prime χ0,K -orbit element bK ∈ K such that χ1,K (bK ) = 0, and bK is not a
proper power in K.

                                                      72
Proof. We first construct a fibered compact special cover with first Betti number at least two. By
virtual fibering, we can choose a finite cover pf : Mf → M with group Gf = π1 (Mf ) ≤ G and a
primitive fibered class χf ∈ H 1 (Mf , Z) [1, 2, 51]. By virtual specialness, we can choose a finite
index compact special subgroup Gv ≤ G [2, 51]. Replace both by their intersection Gs = Gf ∩ Gv .
Let ps : Ms → M be the corresponding cover. Since Gs has finite index in Gf , the image χf (Gs ) is
ds Z for some ds > 0, and
                                      χs = d−1
                                             s χf |Gs : Gs ↠ Z

is primitive and fibered. Thus Ms is again a surface bundle whose return map is a lift of a positive
power of the original pseudo-Anosov return map. Having finite index in the compact special group
Gv , the group Gs is compact special.
    Now choose two noncommensurable loxodromic elements u, v ∈ Gs . Their cyclic subgroups are
quasiconvex in the word-hyperbolic group Gs and have trivial intersection. The subgroups generated
by high powers are also quasiconvex, since they have finite index in these cyclic groups. In the word
metric, all nonidentity elements of ⟨uN ⟩ and ⟨v N ⟩ have arbitrarily large length as N increases. We
may therefore apply the short-element condition in [15, Theorem 1] to these two cyclic subgroups.
For sufficiently large N it yields

                                   F = ⟨uN , v N ⟩ = ⟨uN ⟩ ∗ ⟨v N ⟩ ∼
                                                                    = F2 ,

and also F is quasiconvex. Then the quasiconvex virtual retraction theorem provides a finite index
subgroup G0 ≤ Gs containing F and a retraction r : G0 ↠ F [19, 51]. Let p0 : M0 → Ms be
the corresponding cover. The retraction induces a surjection H1 (G0 , Q) → H1 (F, Q) ∼       = Q2 , so
b1 (M0 ) ≥ 2. If d0 > 0 generates χs (G0 ), then set χ0 = d−1
                                                           0 χs |G0 . This is a primitive fibered class
on M0 .
    The compact special group G0 embeds in a right-angled Artin group A [19]. By [1, Corollary 2.3],
this A has a finite index RFRS subgroup AR . Set GR = G0 ∩ AR . Then GR has finite index in G0
and is RFRS because the RFRS condition is inherited by subgroups [1]. Let pR : MR −→ M0 be
the corresponding cover. If dR > 0 generates χ0 (GR ), define

                                       χR = d−1
                                             R χ0 |GR : GR ↠ Z.

This is the primitive class of the lift of the first fibration. Pullback in rational cohomology is injective
for a finite cover, so
                                           b1 (MR ) ≥ b1 (M0 ) ≥ 2.
    Next we find an integral class with positive and negative orbit evaluations. Choose a connected
fiber SR for the fibration represented by χR , and write f : SR → SR for its pseudo-Anosov return
map. We use the mapping torus description
                                                                             
                                MR = (SR × R)/ (x, s + 1) ∼ (f (x), s) ,

in which the projection θR ([x, s]) = s (mod Z) represents χR . Define the suspension flow by

                                          φtR ([x, s]) = [x, s + t].

The first return map on SR × {0} is then f , and the first return time is one. We call this the
χR -suspension flow.
    If f m (x) = x, then following this flow from [x, 0] for time m traces a closed trajectory cx . We
orient it by increasing flow time. Its projection to the base circle has degree m, so χR ([cx ]) = m > 0.
Let D ⊂ H1 (MR , R) be the closed cone generated by the homology classes of positive closed

                                                     73
trajectories. Let C be the closure of the Thurston fibered cone containing χR . Fried’s theorem
[14, 12] says that

                      D = C ∗ = {v ∈ H1 (MR , R) : ⟨ξ, v⟩ ≥ 0 for every ξ ∈ C}.

In fact, the Thurston norm ∥ · ∥T on H 1 (MR , R) is a norm [47]. Since a supporting linear functional
ℓ for the chosen fibered face satisfies ℓ(ξ) = ∥ξ∥T for every ξ ∈ C, ℓ is strictly positive on C \ {0},
and C ∩ (−C) = {0}. Choose any Euclidean norm on H 1 (MR , R). The intersection of C with its
unit sphere is compact, so ℓ has a positive minimum there, and every sufficiently small perturbation
of ℓ is still nonnegative on C. Identifying linear functionals on H 1 (MR , R) with H1 (MR , R), this
shows that int D is nonempty. Choose a nonzero rational point x ∈ int D. Because b1 (MR ) ≥ 2, the
rational annihilator of x contains a nonzero integral class, and dividing this class by the greatest
common divisor of its coordinates we get a primitive class η ∈ H 1 (MR , Z) with η(x) = 0. Choose
y ∈ H1 (MR , R) with η(y) > 0; then for all sufficiently small t > 0, both x + ty and x − ty lie
in D, and their evaluations under η have opposite signs. If every positive closed trajectory had
nonnegative evaluation, then every finite nonnegative sum of their homology classes would have
nonnegative evaluation, as would every point of its closure D. This contradicts η(x − ty) < 0. The
same argument with the signs reversed rules out nonpositive evaluation on every positive closed
trajectory. There are therefore positive closed trajectories of both strict signs. Replacing each by its
underlying prime trajectory divides the evaluation by a positive integer and preserves its sign, so we
obtain positive prime trajectories c+ , c− with

                                      η(c+ ) > 0,          η(c− ) < 0.

    We now pass to an RFRS cover and find a second fibered class. There are two cases. If η is
fibered, then set p1 = idMR and M1 = MR . Otherwise, Agol’s RFRS theorem provides a finite cover

                                            p1 : M1 −→ MR

such that p∗1 η lies in the closure of a fibered cone [1, Theorem 5.1]. In both cases we set

                                     G1 = π1 (M1 ),          η1 = p∗1 η.

In the first case the associated fibered cone is the one containing η1 . In either case, we choose closed
elevations c′+ and c′− of positive powers of c+ and c− . Then

                                     η1 (c′+ ) > 0,        η1 (c′− ) < 0.

Now choose a rational class in the interior of the indicated fibered cone sufficiently close to η1 that
these two strict inequalities remain true. Take

                                           χ1,1 ∈ H 1 (M1 , Z)

to be the primitive integral class on its positive ray; it is fibered. If d0,1 > 0 generates the image of
p∗1 χR on G1 , then define
                                           χ0,1 = d−1    ∗
                                                    0,1 p1 χR .

Lifting the local product trivializations of the projection θR through p1 shows that θR ◦p1 : M1 → R/Z
is a surface bundle, possibly with disconnected fiber. Its induced homomorphism has image d0,1 Z,
so by the covering space lifting criterion there is a mapping

                                         θ1 : M1 −→ R/d0,1 Z

                                                      74
whose composition with the natural covering R/d0,1 Z → R/Z is θR ◦ p1 . Bundle trivializations lift
through this circle covering, so θ1 is also a surface bundle. Identifying π1 (R/d0,1 Z) with Z, the map
(θ1 )∗ = χ0,1 is surjective. As M1 is connected, the homotopy exact sequence of the bundle implies
that its fiber is connected. So composing θ1 with

                                R/d0,1 Z −→ R/Z,              [s] 7−→ [s/d0,1 ],

gives a surface bundle representing the primitive class χ0,1 .
    Let φetR be the lifted flow on M1 . It satisfies

                                 θ1 (φetR (x)) = θ1 (x) + t     (mod d0,1 Z),
                                                                                         d   t
and its first positive return time to a fiber of θ1 is consequently d0,1 . Hence φt1 = φeR0,1 is the unit-
time suspension for χ0,1 . Since positive time rescaling preserves the oriented trajectories, including
c′+ and c′− , the return map on the connected fiber is a lift of f d0,1 and is hence pseudo-Anosov.
    By Lemma 9.4 for η = χ1,1 , we obtain a positive prime orbit element b ∈ G1 such that χ1,1 (b) = 0.
By Lemma 8.1, b is not a proper power in the mapping torus group G1 . As χ0,1 (b) > 0, the two
classes χ0,1 and χ1,1 are linearly independent.
    Now we pass to a standard characteristic cover. The subgroup G1 has finite index in G, so we
can choose m such that
                                   K = Km (G) ≤ coreG (G1 ) ≤ G1 .
Let pK,1 : MK → M1 be the cover corresponding to K ≤ G1 , and let pK : MK → M be its
composition with the previously chosen finite covers. Thus pK is the standard characteristic cover
corresponding to K = Km (G). For j = 0, 1, let dj,K > 0 be the positive generator of χj,1 (K) and
define
                                  χj,K = d−1j,K χj,1 |K : K ↠ Z.

A finite cover of a surface bundle is a surface bundle after primitive normalization, so both classes
are fibered.
    Let k > 0 be least with bk ∈ K and set bK = bk . Let c be the positive closed trajectory
represented by b. With compatible choices of basepoints and base paths, its lift closes after r
traversals when br ∈ K. By the minimality of k, the element bK = bk therefore represents one
traversal of a connected component of p−1 K,1 (c). This component covers c with degree k and inherits
the positive flow orientation.
    For j = 0, 1, by definition of χj,K ,
                                                1                     k
                                χj,K (bK ) =          χj,1 (bk ) =          χj,1 (b).
                                               dj,K                  dj,K

Since k, d0,K , d1,K > 0, χ1,1 (b) = 0, and χ0,1 (b) > 0, we obtain

                                   χ1,K (bK ) = 0,           χ0,K (bK ) > 0.

So the two normalized classes remain linearly independent.
   It remains to prove that bK is not a proper power in K. As b is not a proper power in G1 , the
centralizer of bk in G1 is ⟨b⟩. Also by the minimality of k we have

                                               K ∩ ⟨b⟩ = ⟨bk ⟩.

Now if z r = bK with z ∈ K and r ≥ 2, then z centralizes bk , so z = bs for some s ∈ Z. Since z ∈ K,
the equality K ∩ ⟨b⟩ = ⟨bk ⟩ forces s = kq, and then bsr = bk forces rq = 1, a contradiction. Thus

                                                        75
bK is not a proper power in K. Finally, suppose that the closed trajectory represented by bK were
an r-fold traversal of a shorter closed orbit, with r ≥ 2. Taking z ∈ π1 (MK ) = K representing one
traversal of that shorter orbit, with the same base path, we would have bK = z r , contradicting the
fact that bK is not a proper power in K. Thus bK represents a positive prime closed trajectory.
This completes the proof.

   Let N be a connected closed 3-manifold and let χ ∈ H 1 (N, Z) be a primitive fibered class.
Choose a surface bundle map p : N → S 1 representing χ, with connected fiber S and basepoint
x ∈ S. The χ-fiber group is                                

The inclusion S ,→ N induces an injective homomorphism whose image is Πχ , so we identify Πχ
with π1 (S, x). For a primitive fibered class χ1,i : Ki → Z, we write

                                                Πi := ker χ1,i

for the χ1,i -fiber group.

Proof. By Theorem 9.5, there are an integer m, the subgroup K1 = Km (G1 ), linearly independent

10     Cellular realization
We use a single marked element in a surface fiber to pass from the completed fiber isomorphism to
the full mapping torus isomorphism. The first lemma determines the completion of a semidirect
product and proves the finite extension statement used at the end of the argument.

   Recall that for a covering p : S ′ → S and a loop α : S 1 → S, an elevation of α is a loop
α′ : S 1 → S ′ such that p ◦ α′ = α ◦ ck , where ck : S 1 → S 1 is the covering of degree k > 0 and k is
the least positive degree for which the chosen lift closes, see [18].

Lemma 10.2. Let Π = π1 (S) for a closed orientable surface S of genus at least two, and let
b ∈ Π \ {1} be an element that is not a proper power in Π. For every sufficiently large m, if km > 0
is least with bkm ∈ Km (Π), then bkm is represented by a nonseparating simple closed curve in the
cover corresponding to Km (Π), and it is not a proper power in Km (Π).

                                                   78
Proof. By Scott’s theorem on subgroups of surface groups [46], with its correction, there is a finite
cover S ′ → S in which the conjugacy class of b has a simple closed elevation α′ . This elevation is
essential, because b is nontrivial and not a proper power in the surface group.
    If α′ is separating, write
                                         S ′ \ α′ = S+
                                                     ′    ′
                                                       ⊔ S− .
Each side has positive genus, because an essential separating curve in a closed surface cannot bound
a disk. Choose homomorphisms φ± : π1 (S±   ′ ) → Z/2 that are nonzero and kill the boundary class.

The two maps agree on the amalgamated boundary subgroup and define a surjection

                                            φ : π1 (S ′ ) → Z/2.

In the corresponding connected double cover, the preimage of each side is connected and α′ has two
lifts, so deleting either one of these lifts leaves the two lifted sides joined along the other. Thus
both lifts are nonseparating. Replacing S ′ by this double cover if necessary, we obtain a finite cover
containing a nonseparating simple elevation α of b.
     Let J ≤ Π be the subgroup defining this cover, and let L = g∈Π gJg −1 be its normal core.
                                                                          T

Choose m0 with Km0 (Π) ≤ L. Then for every m ≥ m0 , each connected elevation α          e to the cover
corresponding to Km (Π) is simple. Denote the covering from this surface to S ′ by p. If the degree
of αe over α is r > 0, then
                                       e = r[α] ̸= 0
                                   p∗ [α]                in H1 (S ′ , Z).
Hence [α]e ̸= 0. Since on an orientable closed surface a simple closed curve is nonseparating when its
homology class is nonzero, every such α   e is nonseparating.
    By construction, the cover corresponding to Km (Π) is normal over S, and its deck group is
transitive on the fiber over a basepoint of the loop representing b and therefore on the connected
elevations of that loop. A lift of α is one such elevation, and every other elevation is a deck translate
of it, so it is also simple and nonseparating. In particular, the elevation beginning at the chosen
sheet has first-return degree km and represents the free homotopy class of bkm . This proves our first
claim.
    Now let K = Km (Π) and take k = km to be least with bk ∈ K. That bk is not a proper power
in K follows exactly as at the end of the proof of Theorem 9.5, using that the centralizer of bk in
the surface group is ⟨b⟩.

    For a closed surface R of genus at least two, the curve graph C(R) [27] has isotopy classes of
essential simple closed curves as vertices. Two distinct vertices are joined by an edge when they
have disjoint representatives. We write dC for its path metric with every edge of length one, and
use xi also for the curve class represented by xi in the surface with fundamental group Ki . For
pseudo-Anosov mapping classes, these distances have a positive linear lower bound in the number of
iterates [27]. Therefore

                           dC (x1 , f1n (x1 )) → ∞,                dC (x2 , g2n (x2 )) → ∞.

Choose n so large that both distances are at least three. Then each pair fills its surface, since a
curve disjoint from both would give a path of length at most two in the curve graph, and all four
curves are nonseparating. The two chosen curves determine the closed cyclic subgroups

Corollary 10.4. Every isomorphism of profinite completions of closed orientable hyperbolic 3-
manifold groups, and every such isomorphism between cocompact lattices in PSL2 (C), is discretely
induced up to profinite inner automorphism.
Proof. For manifolds, Corollary 9.6 provides corresponding characteristic covers with positive prime
suspension orbit elements bi ∈ ker χ1,i . By Lemma 8.1, these elements are not proper powers in the
covering groups and hence are not proper powers in their fiber subgroups ker χ1,i . When applying
Theorem 10.3 we replace b2 by bϵ2 ; inversion preserves this root property. Then the theorem realizes
the isomorphism between the completions of the covering groups. These characteristic covering
groups are normal of finite index, and the conditions of Lemma 10.1(ii) hold by Lemma 3.8, so that
lemma yields the realization on the original groups.
    For cocompact lattices, choose a torsion-free finite index normal subgroup in each lattice by
Selberg’s lemma. A common sufficiently deep standard characteristic level is contained in the chosen
subgroups and is matched by the given profinite isomorphism, and the corresponding quotients of
H3 are closed manifolds. We apply the manifold case to these matched characteristic subgroups,
which are normal of finite index, and conclude with Lemma 10.1(ii), whose conditions hold by
Lemma 3.8.

                                                      81
11       Global lattice
This section uses a standard cusp filling argument. Recall that for a cusp torus T , the peripheral
subgroup is the image of π1 (T ) in the manifold group, defined using a chosen base path, and that
a slope is an unoriented isotopy class of essential simple closed curves on T . After choosing an
orientation, we represent a slope by an element d ∈ π1 (T ) ∼ = Z2 belonging to an integral basis;
                                         −1
changing the orientation replaces d by d .
    For the filling argument we only need each peripheral map to be a profinite unit times an
integral map: after passing to the closed normal subgroup generated by the filling relations, the
unit disappears.

                                                       83
Proposition 11.3. Let M Γ and M ∆ be compact horospherical truncations of the cusped hyperbolic
manifolds with groups Γ and ∆. Use the primitive slopes di , ei and the pairwise distinct orders
ki (n) = ri ℓn from (11.3). For every sufficiently large n, the following statements hold.

  (i) Attaching an orbifold solid torus of generalized coefficient (ki (n), 0) to the ith cusp of M Γ
      produces a compact orientable hyperbolic orbifold OΓ,n with

                                               π1orb (OΓ,n ) ∼
                                                             = Γn .

     The corresponding filling of M ∆ produces O∆,n with orbifold group ∆n .

 (ii) The singular locus consists exactly of the filling singular closed geodesic κi,n . The local group
      along κi,n is cyclic of order ki (n), and the image of di , respectively ei , has exact order ki (n)
      in the orbifold group.

 (iii) Every nontrivial finite subgroup of Γn , respectively ∆n , is conjugate into the local cyclic group
       of some filling singular closed geodesic.

 (iv) Every orbifold isometry
                                              Fn : OΓ,n −→ O∆,n
      maps the singular geodesic of order ki (n) to the singular geodesic of the same order. After
      deleting invariant open tubes around all singular closed geodesic and adjoining product collars,
      its restriction is a homeomorphism

                                               F n : M Γ −→ M ∆

      that takes the slope di to ei up to sign.

 (v) Let
                                        qn : Γ ↠ Γ n ,          qn′ : ∆ ↠ ∆n
      be the filling maps. Choose basepoints outside the singular closed geodesic tubes and compatible
      base paths. If fn = (Fn )∗ and hn = (F n )∗ then, after replacing fn by a discrete inner conjugate,

     (11.4)                                       fn qn = qn′ hn .

Proof. In the basis consisting of the primitive slope and a longitude on the ith cusp, the generalized
coefficient (ki (n), 0) makes di the orbifold meridian with cone angle 2π/ki (n). Because all ki (n)
tend to infinity, the simultaneous orbifold Dehn filling theorem applies for all sufficiently large n [6]
and produces the hyperbolic orbifolds in part (i). Since the local filling model is

                                            (D2 × S 1 )/Cki (n) ,

where the cyclic group Cki (n) of order ki (n) acts by rotations on the disk and trivially on the circle,
the singular locus in the attached solid torus is exactly its singular closed geodesic and has effective
local order ki (n). The complement of the attached solid tori is the original manifold truncation
and has no singular points. This proves the statement about the singular locus in part (ii). Since
developability makes each local group inject into the orbifold fundamental group, the orbifold van
Kampen theorem yields
                                                   k (n)
                              π1orb (OΓ,n ) = Γ/⟨⟨d11      , . . . , dks s (n) ⟩⟩Γ = Γn ,

                                                     84
and similarly for ∆n , which also shows that the slope image has exact order ki (n).
    Let H be a nontrivial finite subgroup of either orbifold group. By the Cartan fixed point theorem,
its action on H3 has a common fixed point, and the point projects to the singular locus, since
regular points have trivial stabilizer. By part (ii), its local stabilizer is the cyclic group of a singular
geodesic, and H is conjugate into that group. This proves part (iii).
    Since the numbers ki (n) = ri ℓn are pairwise distinct, Fn maps each singular geodesic to the
singular geodesic with the same label. Choose pairwise disjoint sufficiently small closed tubes around
the singular geodesics in the orbifold, and use their images under Fn as the corresponding tubes in
the second orbifold. Denote the underlying solid tori by Vi and Vi′ , respectively. Choose the tubes
small enough to lie inside the corresponding attached solid tori on both sides. The regions between
their boundaries and the original filling boundaries are product collars. Thus the restriction of Fn
to the complements of the tube interiors, followed by the collar identifications, is a homeomorphism

                                            F n : M Γ −→ M ∆ .

The restriction to the tubes yields a homeomorphism of the underlying solid tori Vi → Vi′ , whose
boundary restriction maps

                                                              ker π1 (∂Vi′ ) → π1 (Vi′ ) .
                                                                                       
                       ker π1 (∂Vi ) → π1 (Vi )       onto

These infinite cyclic kernels are generated by the primitive disk-bounding meridians. Under the
collar identifications their generators represent di and ei , respectively, so F n takes di to ei up to
sign. This proves part (iv).
    We obtain the filling of M Γ by attaching the orbifold solid tori along their boundary tori, and we
use the same construction on the ∆ side. Choose the collar identifications so that, after inclusion into
the filled orbifolds, they are homotopic to the original filling inclusions. Under these identifications,
F n is induced by the restriction of Fn to the tube complements. Hence the two composites from
M Γ to O∆,n are homotopic. Their induced homomorphisms satisfy fn qn = Inn(bn )qn′ hn for some
bn ∈ ∆n . Replacing fn by Inn(b−1 n )fn we obtain (11.4).

Proof of Theorem 2.9. By Selberg’s lemma, choose torsion-free finite index normal subgroups N1 ⊴f
Γ and N2 ⊴f ∆, and choose an integer m large enough that
                            H1 = Km (Γ) ≤ N1 ,          H2 = Km (∆) ≤ N2 .
Then the Hi are torsion-free characteristic finite index subgroups and by Lemma 3.1 we have
Φ(Hb 1) = Hb 2 . Finite index subgroups inherit their full profinite topologies, so the restriction is an
isomorphism H    b1 → H b 2.
    The finite volume hyperbolic manifolds Mi = H3 /Hi are either both closed or both cusped,
by the cohomological argument in the proof of Corollary 11.5. Applying Corollary 10.4 in the
closed case and Corollary 11.4 in the cusped case, we obtain an isomorphism h : H1 → H2 inducing
the restricted map up to a profinite inner automorphism. Applying Lemma 10.1(ii) to the finite
extensions H1 ⊴f Γ and H2 ⊴f ∆, which is possible by Lemma 3.8, we obtain

                                            Φ = Inn(u) ◦ fb
for an isomorphism f : Γ → ∆.
    Since Mostow–Prasad rigidity for finite volume hyperbolic orbifolds realizes f , up to a discrete
inner automorphism, by an isometry, possibly reversing orientation [34, 39], we obtain the realization
of Φ described in the theorem and the isomorphism of the discrete groups, as well as the surjectivity
of Out(Γ) → Out(Γ).  b
    Finally, for injectivity, let α ∈ Aut(Γ) and suppose α

12       Compact core realization
In this section we assume that equivariant isomorphisms of residual complexes are given at a cofinal
family of characteristic quotients. For the conclusion about the groups it is enough to have a
graph whose inclusion is surjective on fundamental groups. If we keep the full simplex face residual
complex, we also obtain a PL homeomorphism of the chosen compact cores. These conclusions do
not use Theorem 2.9.

Schottky obstruction
Finally, we give an elementary fact which is an easy consequence of [40].



                                                      90
Proposition 12.8. For every r ≥ 2 there is a classical Schottky group Γ < PSL2 (C) of rank
r and an automorphism Ψ ∈ Aut(Γ)
                               b for which there are no w ∈ Γ   b and f ∈ Aut(Γ) such that
Ψ = Inn(w) ◦ f .
             b

Proof. Let Γ be any classical Schottky group of rank r ≥ 2, and choose a free basis x1 , . . . , xr . Then
b is the free profinite group on this basis [40]. Choose λ ∈ Z
Γ                                                              b × \ {1, −1}. By the universal property,
the assignments
                             Ψλ (x1 ) = xλ1 ,    Ψλ (xj ) = xj (2 ≤ j ≤ r)
define a continuous endomorphism of Γ.b Continuous homomorphisms preserve profinite powers, so
the map Ψλ−1 is its inverse and Ψλ is an automorphism.
                                 b ab ∼
    On continuous abelianization Γ    =Zb r , this automorphism induces

                                        (Ψλ )ab = diag(λ, 1, . . . , 1).

Suppose that Ψλ = Inn(w) ◦ fb for some w ∈ Γb and f ∈ Aut(Γ). Inner automorphisms act trivially
on abelianization, whereas f induces a matrix fab ∈ GLr (Z). Taking determinants therefore gives

                                          λ = det(fab ) ∈ {1, −1},

a contradiction.

Corollary 12.9. Let Γ be as in Proposition 12.8, acting cocompactly on a connected locally finite
directed multigraph X with finite vertex stabilizers, and fix a finite labelled distinguished family S.
Then
                                               ∼
                                               =                        
                             Out(Γ ↷ X, S) −  → Out Γ  b↷D   b Γ (X), Sb ,

although Out(Γ) → Out(Γ)
                      b is not surjective.

Proof. The action statement is Theorem 6.11. The automorphism of Proposition 12.8 shows that
Out(Γ) → Out(Γ)b is not surjective.
